.

5.7.2
Indexed-colorings

For converting traditional colorings to string-colorings, we introduce the following indexed-colorings.

Definition 47
.

[
45
]
There are three
indexed-colorings
of graphs as follows:

1. Suppose that a graph
G
admits a proper vertex
k
-coloring
f:V(G)\rightarrow[1,k]
, such that the vertex set
V(G)=\bigcup^{k}_{i=1}V_{i}(G)
, and each vertex
u_{i,j}\in V_{i}(G)
is colored with color
f(u_{i,j})=i_{j}
for
j\in[1,n_{i}]
and
i\in[1,k]
, where
n_{i}=|V_{i}(G)|
, we have vertex color sets

C_{i}(G)=\{f(x_{i,j}):x_{i,j}\in V_{i}(G)\}=\{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n_{i}}\},~i\in[1,k]

where the color
i_{j}
is called
j
th color
i
. For each edge
uv\in E(G)
, the
edge induced-color

f^{*}(uv)=F\langle f(u),f(v)\rangle
defined by one of
addition

a_{i}(+)b_{j}=(a+b)_{i+j}=F\langle f(u),f(v)\rangle
,
multiplication

a_{i}(\cdot)b_{j}=(a\cdot b)_{ij}=F\langle f(u),f(v)\rangle
and
subtraction

a_{i}(-)b_{j}=|a-b|_{|i-j|}=F\langle f(u),f(v)\rangle
, where two vertex colors
f(u)=a_{i}
and
f(v)=b_{j}
. We call
f
a
proper vertex indexed-coloring
, and
\langle f,f^{*}\rangle
an
indexed proper total coloring
.

2. Suppose that a graph
G
admits a proper edge
k
-coloring
g:E(G)\rightarrow[1,k]
, such that the edge set
E(G)=\bigcup^{k}_{r=1}E_{r}(G)
, and each edge
u_{r,j}v_{r,j}\in E_{r}(G)
is colored with color
r_{j}
for
j\in[1,s_{r}]
and
r\in[1,k]
, where
s_{r}=|E_{r}(G)|
, we have edge color sets

EC_{r}(G)=\{g(u_{r,j}v_{r,j}):u_{r,j}v_{r,j}\in E_{r}(G)\}=\{r_{1},r_{2},\dots,r_{s_{r}}\},~r\in[1,k]

For each vertex
x
of
G
with its neighbor set
N_{ei}(x)=\{y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{d}\}
, the
vertex induced-color
is as

g^{*}(x)=A[r_{1},r_{2},\dots,r_{d}]_{B[j(r_{1}),j(r_{2}),\dots,j(r_{d})]}

(225)

for
g(xy_{r_{i},j})=(r_{i})_{j(r_{i})}
with
xy_{r_{i},j}\in E_{r_{i}}(G)
and
r_{i}\in[1,k]
, where
A[r_{1},r_{2},\dots,r_{d}]
is a function of colors
r_{i}
with
r_{i}\in[1,k]
, and
B[j(r_{1}),j(r_{2}),\dots,j(r_{d})]
is a function of
j(r_{i})
th color
r_{i}
with
i\in[1,d]
, as well as degree
d=\textrm{deg}(x)
. We call
g
a
proper edge indexed-coloring
, and
\langle g,g^{*}\rangle
a
proper total indexed-coloring
.

3. Suppose that a
(p,q)
-graph
G
admits a proper total
M
-coloring
h:V(G)\cup E(G)\rightarrow[1,M]
, such that the total set
V(G)\cup E(G)=\bigcup^{M}_{j=1}S_{j}(G)
, and each element
w_{j,k}\in S_{j}(G)
is colored with color
j_{k}
for
k\in[1,t_{j}]
and
j\in[1,M]
, where
t_{j}=|S_{j}(G)|
and
p+q=\sum^{M}_{j=1}t_{j}
, we have the edge color sets

TC_{j}(G)=\{h(w_{j,k}):w_{j,k}\in S_{j}(G)\}=\{j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{t_{j}}\},~j\in[1,M]

We call
h
a
proper total indexed-coloring
.
\square

Remark 34
.

[
45
]
In the proper edge
k
-coloring of Definition
47
, the vertex induced-color
g^{*}(x)
defined in Eq.(
225
) can be as

A[r_{1},r_{2},\dots,r_{d}]=\sum^{d}_{t=1}r_{t}
and
B[j(r_{1}),j(r_{2}),\dots,j(r_{d})]=\sum^{d}_{t=1}j(r_{t})
, or

A[r_{1},r_{2},\dots,r_{d}]=\prod^{d}_{t=1}r_{t}
and
B[j(r_{1}),j(r_{2}),\dots,j(r_{d})]=\prod^{d}_{t=1}j(r_{t})
, or

A[r_{1},r_{2},\dots,r_{d}]=\sum^{d}_{t=1}r_{t}
and
B[j(r_{1}),j(r_{2}),\dots,j(r_{d})]=\prod^{d}_{t=1}j(r_{t})
, or

A[r_{1},r_{2},\dots,r_{d}]=\prod^{d}_{t=1}r_{t}
and
B[j(r_{1}),j(r_{2}),\dots,j(r_{d})]=\sum^{d}_{t=1}j(r_{t})
, and so on.

There are the following characteristics of Definition
47
for the computational complexity of topological coding:

(i) The colors in each of color sets
C_{i}(G)
,
EC_{r}(G)
and
TC_{j}(G)
differ from each other, since their indexes are different from each other. Thereby, a
W
-constraint indexed-coloring induces more
W
-constraint indexed-colorings, where
W
-type
\in\{
proper vertex, proper edge, proper total
\}
.

(ii) The proper vertex indexed-coloring and the proper edge indexed-coloring both produce proper total indexed-colorings of
G
by the vertex induced-colors and the edge induced-colors. There are many ways to produce the vertex induced-color
g^{*}(x)=A[r_{1},r_{2},\dots,r_{d}]_{B[j(r_{1}),j(r_{2}),\dots,j(r_{d})]}
and the edge induced-color
f^{*}(uv)=F(f(u),f(v))
.

(iii) See an indexed proper total
M
-coloring
h
of a
(p,q)
-graph
G
defined in Definition
47
. Let
a_{j_{1}},a_{j_{2}},\dots,a_{j_{t_{j}}}
be a permutation of colors
j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{t_{j}}
of
TC_{j}(G)
, so the elements of
S_{j}(G)
defined in Definition
47
can be colored by
(t_{j})!
different ways. And moreover, there are
\Omega
different indexed-colorings for the
(p,q)
-graph
G
based on an indexed proper total
M
-coloring
h
, where
\Omega=\prod^{M}_{j=1}(t_{j})!
. Suppose that a
(p,q)
-graph
G
admits
N_{tc}
different proper total indexed-colorings, then we get
\Omega\cdot N_{tc}
different proper total indexed-colorings of
G
. It will help us resist the attack of quantum computation by producing huge amount of topological codes.

(iv) Many of
\Omega\cdot N_{tc}
different proper total indexed-colorings of
G
are distinguishing colorings, so are those proper total indexed-colorings induced by the proper vertex indexed-coloring and the proper edge indexed-coloring.

(v) As
k=\chi(G),\chi\,^{\prime}(G)
and
M=\chi\,^{\prime\prime}(G)
, determining the colorings defined in Definition
47
is not easy, even difficult, since there are two longstanding conjectures:

{\begin{split}&\textrm{Reed's conjecture: }~\chi(G)\leq\left\lceil\frac{\Delta(G)+1+K(G)}{2}\right\rceil,\\
&\textrm{Behzad and Vizing's conjecture: }~\chi\,^{\prime\prime}(G)\leq\Delta(G)+2\end{split}}

proposed by Bruce Reed (1998), Behzad (1965), Vizing (1964), respectively.
\parallel

Definition 48
.

An
i
-
color set

S_{i}=V_{i}\cup E_{i}
consists of
V_{i}=\{u:f(u)=i,u\in V(G)\}
and
E_{i}=\{xy:f(xy)=i,xy\in E(G)\}
foe a graph
G
. If
\big||S_{i}|-|S_{j}|\big|\leq 1
for any pair of color sets
S_{i}
and
S_{j}

(1\leq i,j\leq k)
, we say that
f
is a
k
-
equitable total coloring
of the graph
G
.
Straightly, the number

\chi\,^{\prime\prime}_{e}(G)=\min\{k:\text{ over all
$k$-equitable total colorings of }G\}

is called
equitable total chromatic number
of the graph
G
.
\square

Conjecture 6
.

(Weifan Wang, 2002) For every graph
G
, then
\chi\,^{\prime\prime}_{e}(G)\leq\Delta(G)+2
.

Remark 35
.

Let
\textbf{{H}}_{total}=(H_{1},H_{2},\dots,H_{n})
be a
graph base
, where each connected graph
H_{i}
with
\chi\,^{\prime\prime}(H_{i})\leq\Delta(H_{i})+2
has a property
P_{rop}(H_{i})
for
i\in[1,m]
, and each connected graph
H_{j}
with a property
P_{rop}(H_{j})
holds
\chi\,^{\prime\prime}(H_{j})\leq\Delta(H_{j})+2
for
j\in[m+1,n]
. Each connected graph
H_{r}
admits a proper total coloring
f_{r}:V(H_{r})\cup E(H_{r})\rightarrow[1,\chi\,^{\prime\prime}(H_{r})]
for
r\in[1,n]
.

Let
G_{i,1},G_{i,2},\dots,G_{i,M}
be the
i
th permutation of graphs
a_{1}H_{1},a_{2}H_{2},\dots,a_{n}H_{n}
with
M=\sum^{n}_{k=1}a_{k}
and
i\in[1,M!]
.

We do the vertex-coinciding operation to each graph
G_{i,j}
for
j\in[1,M]
and
i\in[1,M!]
in the following way: Let
u_{r}\in V(G_{i,r})
be a
maximal degree vertex
with
\textrm{deg}_{G_{i,r}}(u_{r})=\Delta(G_{i,r})
for
r\in[1,M]
, and without loss of generality,
f_{r}(u_{r})=1
. We vertex-coincide a maximal degree vertex
u_{r}
with a maximal degree vertex
u_{r+1}
into one vertex
u_{r}\odot u_{r+1}
, define a new total coloring
f^{*}
for the graph
G_{i,r}[\odot]G_{i,r+1}
as:
f^{*}(u_{r})=f^{*}(u_{r+1})=1
, and recolor edges
u_{r+1}v_{r+1,s}
with
f^{*}(u_{r+1}v_{r+1,s})=\Delta(H_{r})+2+s
for
s\in[1,\Delta(H_{r+1})]
if
\textrm{deg}_{H_{r}}(u_{r})\geq\textrm{deg}_{H_{r+1}}(u_{r+1})
, so

f^{*}(u_{r+1}v_{r+1,\Delta(G_{i,r+1})})=\Delta(G_{i,r})+2+\Delta(G_{i,r+1})=\Delta(G_{i,r}[\odot]G_{i,r+1})+2

and keep the colors of other vertices and edges in the graph
G_{i,r}[\odot]G_{i,r+1}
as they were. The vertex-coincided graph
G
is denoted as

G=G_{i,1}[\odot]G_{i,2}[\odot]\cdots[\odot]G_{i,M}=[\odot]^{n}_{k=1}a_{k}H_{k}

and

E(G)=\bigcup^{n}_{k=1}a_{k}E(H_{k})

(226)

It is allowed that the maximal degree vertex
u_{1}
is vertex-coincided with each maximal degree vertex
u_{r}
for
r\in[2,n]
such that the vertex-coincided graph
G=[\odot]^{n}_{k=1}a_{k}H_{k}
has

\Delta(G)=\sum^{n}_{k=1}\Delta(G_{i,k})
. Thereby, the vertex-coincided graph
G=[\odot]^{n}_{k=1}a_{k}H_{k}
is simple and connected, and admits a proper total coloring
f^{*}
holding

\max\{f^{*}(w):w\in V(G)\cup E(G)\}\leq\Delta(G)+2

(227)

and holds Eq.(
226
). Then we get a
graphic lattice

\textbf{{L}}([\odot]Z^{0}\textbf{{H}}_{total})=\Big\{[\odot]^{n}_{k=1}a_{k}H_{k}:a_{k}\in Z^{0},H_{k}\in\textbf{{H}}_{total}\Big\}

(228)

with
M=\sum^{n}_{k=1}a_{k}\geq 1
. As
a_{i}\geq 1
and
a_{j}\geq 1
for
i\neq j
, the connected graph
G\in\textbf{{L}}([\odot]Z^{0}\textbf{{H}}_{total})
does not hold two properties
P_{rop}(H_{i})
and
P_{rop}(H_{j})
at the same time. In other word, if there are no necessary and sufficient conditions for Behzad and Vizing’s conjecture, then the number of connected graphs in the graph base
\textbf{{H}}_{total}
will get bigger and bigger.
\parallel

Problem 22
.

By Remark
35
, it is natural to guess: “A connected graph
G
holds
\chi\,^{\prime\prime}(G)\leq\Delta(G)+2
if and only if
G
can be vertex-split into mutually edge-disjoint connected graphs
G_{1},G_{2},\dots,G_{m}
such that each connected graph
G_{i}
holds
\chi\,^{\prime\prime}(G_{i})\leq\Delta(G_{i})+2
for
i\in[1,m]
”.

We, also, can conjecture: “A connected graph
G
holds
\chi\,^{\prime\prime}(G)\leq\Delta(G)+2
if and only if there is a graph base
\textbf{{H}}_{total}=(H_{1},H_{2},\dots,H_{n})
such that the connected graph
G
belongs to a graphic lattice
\textbf{{L}}([\odot]Z^{0}\textbf{{H}}_{total})
defined in Eq.(
228
)”.

5.7.3
Indexed-colorings of planar graphs

Definition 49
.

[
45
]
Suppose that a planar graph
T
admits a proper vertex
4
-coloring
g
, so its vertex set
V(T)
can be divided into four subsets, namely,
V(T)=\bigcup^{4}_{k=1}V_{k}(T)
such that each vertex
x_{k,j}\in V_{k}(T)
is colored with color
k_{j}
for
j\in[1,m_{k}]
and
k\in[1,4]
, where
m_{k}=|V_{k}(T)|
. So we have four vertex color sets

C_{k}(T)=\{g(x_{k,j}):x_{k,j}\in V_{k}(T)\}=\{k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{m_{k}}\},~k\in[1,4]

And for two ends of each edge
uv\in E(G)
colored with
g(u)=a_{i}
and
g(v)=b_{j}
, we define the edge color
g(uv)
with one of
indexed operations
:
indexed addition

a_{i}(+)b_{j}=(a+b)_{(i+j)}
,
indexed multiplication

a_{i}(\cdot)b_{j}=ab_{ij}
and
indexed subtraction

a_{i}(-)b_{j}=|a-b|_{|i-j|}
. Then the proper vertex
4
-coloring
g
and the indexed operations induce a
total indexed-coloring
of
T
.
\square

Example 28
.

In Fig.
25
, a
public-key graph

G
corresponds a
private-key graph

H_{1}
, and a topological signature authentication
T_{1}=\langle G,H_{1}\rangle
; another topological signature authentication
T_{2}=\langle G,H_{2}\rangle
made by the public-key graph
G
and the private-key graph
H_{2}
. There is a cycle
C_{6}
in
G
,
H_{1}
,
T_{1}
,
H_{2}
and
T_{2}
. We have

(i) the public-key graph
G
admits a proper vertex 4-coloring
f
;

(ii) each private-key graph
H_{i}
admits a proper vertex 4-coloring
g_{i}
for
i=1,2
;

(iii) each topological signature authentication
T_{i}
admits a proper vertex 4-coloring
F_{i}
for
i=1,2
;

(iv) the cycle
C_{6}
admits a proper vertex 4-coloring
h
.

Moreover, we have

V(G)=\bigcup^{4}_{i=1}V_{i}(G)
, then the vertex color sets
f(V_{1}(G))=\{1_{1},1_{2},1_{3},1_{4}\}
,
f(V_{2}(G))=\{2_{1}\}
,
f(V_{3}(G))=\{3_{1},3_{2}\}
and
f(V_{4}(G))=\{4_{1},4_{2},4_{3}\}
.

V(H_{1})=\bigcup^{4}_{i=1}V_{i}(H_{1})
, then the vertex color sets
g_{1}(V_{1}(H_{1}))=\{1_{2},1_{3},1_{4}\}
,
g_{1}(V_{2}(H_{1}))=\{2_{2}\}
,
g_{1}(V_{3}(H_{1}))=\{3_{2},3_{3}\}
and
g_{1}(V_{4}(H_{1}))=\{4_{2},4_{3}\}
.

V(T_{1})=\bigcup^{4}_{i=1}V_{i}(T_{1})
, then the vertex color sets
F(V_{1}(T_{1}))=\{1_{1},1_{2},1_{3},1_{4}\}
,
F(V_{2}(T_{1}))=\{2_{1},2_{2}\}
,
F(V_{3}(T_{1}))=\{3_{1},3_{2},3_{3}\}
and
F(V_{4}(T_{1}))=\{4_{1},4_{2},4_{3}\}
.

Figure 25:
Examples for the topological signature authentication based on maximal planar graphs, cited from
[
45
]
.

Topological matrices.
We get the following Topcode-matrices
T_{code}(G,f)_{3\times 20}
shown in Eq.(
230
),
T_{code}(H_{1},g_{1})_{3\times 15}
shown in Eq.(
231
),
T_{code}(C_{6},h)_{3\times 6}

Remark 36
.

[
35
]
In Definition
49
, we have defined the edge color of an edge
uv
with two ends colors
g(u)=a_{i}
and
g(v)=b_{j}
under a proper vertex
4
-coloring
g
. Furthermore, we have

Oper
-1.

Three proper operations
: Addition
a_{i}(+)b_{j}=(a+b)_{(i+j)}
, multiplication
a_{i}(\cdot)b_{j}=ab_{ij}
, subtraction
a_{i}(-)b_{j}=|a-b|_{|i-j|}
.

Oper
-2.

Mixed operation 1
:
a_{i}(+\times)b_{j}=(a+b)_{ij}
,
a_{i}(+|-|)b_{j}=(a+b)_{|i-j|}
.

Oper
-3.

Mixed operation 2
:
a_{i}(\times+)b_{j}=(a\cdot b)_{i+j}
,
a_{i}(\times|-|)b_{j}=(a\cdot b)_{|i-j|}
.

Oper
-4.

Mixed operation 3
:
a_{i}(|-|\times)b_{j}=|a-b|_{ij}
,
a_{i}(|-|+)b_{j}=|a-b|_{i+j}
.

Oper
-5.

Operation based on Klein four-group
: Let
0:=1
,
a:=2
,
b:=3
,
c:=4
in the table
K^{+}_{\textrm{lein}}
shown in Eq.(
235
). We define
(K^{+}_{\textrm{lein}})
addition with the commutative law
r_{i}(K^{+}_{\textrm{lein}})s_{j}=s_{j}(K^{+}_{\textrm{lein}})r_{i}
in the following computations:
1_{i}(K^{+}_{\textrm{lein}})1_{j}=1_{i+j}
,
1_{i}(K^{+}_{\textrm{lein}})2_{j}=2_{i+j}
,
1_{i}(K^{+}_{\textrm{lein}})3_{j}=3_{i+j}
,
1_{i}(K^{+}_{\textrm{lein}})4_{j}=4_{i+j}
;
2_{i}(K^{+}_{\textrm{lein}})2_{j}=2_{i+j}
,
2_{i}(K^{+}_{\textrm{lein}})3_{j}=4_{i+j}
,
2_{i}(K^{+}_{\textrm{lein}})4_{j}=3_{i+j}
;
3_{i}(K^{+}_{\textrm{lein}})3_{j}=1_{i+j}
,
3_{i}(K^{+}_{\textrm{lein}})4_{j}=2_{i+j}
; and
4_{i}(K^{+}_{\textrm{lein}})4_{j}=1_{i+j}
; refer to Fig.
26
(a) and (a-1).

We define
(K^{\times}_{\textrm{lein}})
multiplication with the commutative law
r_{i}(K^{\times}_{\textrm{lein}})s_{j}=s_{j}(K^{\times}_{\textrm{lein}})r_{i}
as follows:
1_{i}(K^{\times}_{\textrm{lein}})1_{j}=1_{ij}
,
1_{i}(K^{\times}_{\textrm{lein}})2_{j}=1_{ij}
,
1_{i}(K^{\times}_{\textrm{lein}})3_{j}=1_{ij}
,
1_{i}(K^{\times}_{\textrm{lein}})4_{j}=1_{ij}
;
2_{i}(K^{\times}_{\textrm{lein}})2_{j}=2_{ij}
,
2_{i}(K^{\times}_{\textrm{lein}})3_{j}=3_{ij}
,
2_{i}(K^{\times}_{\textrm{lein}})4_{j}=4_{ij}
;
3_{i}(K^{\times}_{\textrm{lein}})3_{j}=3_{ij}
,
3_{i}(K^{\times}_{\textrm{lein}})4_{j}=2_{ij}
; and
4_{i}(K^{\times}_{\textrm{lein}})4_{j}=3_{ij}
; refer to Fig.
26
(b) and (b-1).
\parallel

Remark 37
.

[
35
]
Let
K_{\textrm{lein}}=\{0,a,b,c\}
be
Klein four-group
with addition “
+
” in
K^{+}_{\textrm{lein}}
shown in Eq.(
235
). The Klein four-group can be extended to a
finite field
, called the
Klein field
, where multiplication is added as a second operation, with
0
as the zero element and
a
as the identity element. The multiplication table is
K^{\times}_{\textrm{lein}}
shown in Eq.(
235
). Multiplication “
\times
” and addition “
+
” obey the distributive law.

\centering K^{+}_{\textrm{lein}}=\begin{array}[]{c|cccc|c}+&0&a&b&c&\textrm{substitution}\\
\hline\cr 0&0&a&b&c\\
a&a&0&c&b&(1,2)(3,4)\\
b&b&c&0&a&(1,3)(2,4)\\
c&c&b&a&0&(1,4)(2,3)\end{array}\qquad K^{\times}_{\textrm{lein}}=\begin{array}[]{c|ccccc}\times&0&a&b&c\\
\hline\cr 0&0&0&0&0\\
a&0&a&b&c\\
b&0&b&c&a\\
c&0&c&a&b\end{array}\@add@centering

(235)

A Klein four-group, also, is a normal subgroup of the
alternating group

A_{4}
and of the
symmetric group

S_{4}
over four letters.
\parallel

and

shown in Eq.(

).

5.8
Hanzi-based strings

Since Hanzi-graphs admit many colorings/labelings defined in this article, applying them to the field of information will greatly help people of using Chinese characters to be unimpeded in digital finance and personal privacy protection, on the other hands, Hanzi-graphs can be feeded into a computer by speaking, writing and keyboard inputs in Chinese
[
37
,
35
,
40
,
60
,
67
]
. Hanzi-graphs are planar graphs, so they are easy to be imputed into computer by many techniques.

5.8.1
Planar graphs and Hanzi-based strings

Let
C_{sent}=H_{a_{1}b_{1}c_{1}d_{1}}H_{a_{2}b_{2}c_{2}d_{2}}\cdots H_{a_{n}b_{n}c_{n}d_{n}}
be a Hanzi-based string, where each Hanzi-graph
H_{a_{i}b_{i}c_{i}d_{i}}
for
i\in[1,n]
is defined in
[
25
]
. Suppose that each Hanzi-graph
H_{a_{i}b_{i}c_{i}d_{i}}
for
i\in[1,n]
admits
m_{i}
colorings
f_{i,1},f_{i,2},\dots,f_{i,m_{i}}
defined on sets of numbers, then we have
Hanzi-graph Topcode-matrices

T_{code}(H_{a_{i}b_{i}c_{i}d_{i}},f_{i,j})_{3\times q_{i}}=(X^{j}_{a_{i}b_{i}c_{i}d_{i}},E^{j}_{a_{i}b_{i}c_{i}d_{i}},Y^{j}_{a_{i}b_{i}c_{i}d_{i}})^{T}

(236)

where the edge number
q_{i}=|E(H_{a_{i}b_{i}c_{i}d_{i}})|
for
j\in[1,m_{i}]
and
i\in[1,n]
.

Each Hanzi-graph Topcode-matrix
T_{code}(H_{a_{i}b_{i}c_{i}d_{i}},f_{i,j})_{3\times q_{i}}
produces
(3q_{i})!
different Hanzi-based strings in total, then each Hanzi-graph
H_{a_{i}b_{i}c_{i}d_{i}}
produces
m_{i}(3q_{i})!
different number-based strings.

Thereby, the Chinese sentence
C_{sent}
provides us

n_{string}(C_{sent})=\sum^{n}_{i=1}m_{i}(3q_{i})!

different Hanzi-based strings in total.

Figure 27:
A Hanzi-based string
H_{an}=H_{3907}H_{4582}H_{4043}H_{5414}H_{4147}H_{2083}H_{5341}H_{2382}
.

There are
40320=8!
sentences made by the Chinese letters
H_{3907}
,
H_{4582}
,
H_{4043}
,
H_{5414}
,
H_{4147}
,
H_{2083}
,
H_{5341}
, and
H_{2382}
shown in Fig.

, in total; part of them are shown in Fig.
54
.

Example 29
.

Fig.
28
shows us some colorings and labelings of the Hanzi-graphs
H_{3907}
,
H_{4582}
,

,

,

,
H_{2083}
,
H_{5341}
and
H_{2382}
shown in Fig.
27
as follows:

(H-1) the Hanzi-graph
H_{3907}
admits an
edge-magic total labeling

f_{1}
holding the edge-magic constraint

f_{1}(u)+f_{1}(uv)+f_{1}(v)=10

for each edge
uv\in E(H_{3907})
, such that the vertex color set
f_{1}(V(H_{3907}))=[0,6]
and the edge color set
f_{1}(E(H_{3907}))=[1,6]
;

(H-2) the Hanzi-graph
H_{4582}
admits a non-set-ordered odd-graceful labeling
f_{2}
holding each odd number
f_{2}(uv)=|f_{2}(u)-f_{2}(v)|
for each edge
uv\in E(H_{4582})
, such that the vertex color set
f_{2}(V(H_{4582}))\subset[0,17]
and the edge color set
f_{2}(E(H_{4582}))=[1,17]^{o}
;

(H-3) the Hanzi-graph
H_{4043}
admits an edge-difference total labeling
f_{3}
holding the edge-difference constraint

f_{3}(uv)+|f_{3}(u)-f_{3}(v)|=6

for each edge
uv\in E(H_{4043})
, such that the vertex color set
f_{3}(V(H_{4043}))=[0,5]
and the edge color set
f_{3}(E(H_{4043}))=[1,5]
;

(H-4) the Hanzi-graph
H_{5414}
admits a felicitous-difference total labeling
f_{4}
holding the felicitous-difference constraint

|f_{4}(u)+f_{4}(v)-f_{4}(uv)|=2

for each edge
uv\in E(H_{5414})
, such that the vertex color set
f_{4}(V(H_{5414}))=[0,7]
and the edge color set
f_{4}(E(H_{5414}))=[1,7]
;

(H-5) the Hanzi-graph
H_{4147}
admits an all-odd set-ordered graceful-difference total labeling
f_{5}
holding the graceful-difference constraint

\big||f_{5}(v)-f_{5}(u)|-f_{5}(uv)\big|=1

for each edge
uv\in E(H_{4147})
, such that the vertex color set
f_{5}(V(H_{4147}))=[1,9]^{o}
and the edge color set
f_{5}(E(H_{4147}))=[1,11]^{o}
;

(H-6) the Hanzi-graph
H_{2083}

f_{6}
holding
f_{6}(uv)=f_{6}(u)+f_{6}(v)~(\bmod~7)
for each edge
uv\in E(H_{2083})
, such that the vertex color set
f_{6}(V(H_{2083}))=[1,9]^{o}
and the edge color set
f_{6}(E(H_{2083}))=[1,11]^{o}

(H-7) the Hanzi-graph
H_{5341}

f_{7}

About planar graphs and Hanzi-graphs, we have the following jobs to do:

(i)
Hanzi-graphs
\rightarrow
planar graphs
. Let

\textbf{{H}}_{an}=(H_{a_{1}b_{1}c_{1}d_{1}},H_{a_{2}b_{2}c_{2}d_{2}},\dots,H_{a_{n}b_{n}c_{n}d_{n}})

(237)

be a
Hanzi-graph base
made by mutually disjoint Hanzi-graphs
H_{a_{1}b_{1}c_{1}d_{1}}
,
H_{a_{2}b_{2}c_{2}d_{2}}
,
\dots
,
H_{a_{n}b_{n}c_{n}d_{n}}
, where each Hanzi-graph
H_{a_{i}b_{i}c_{i}d_{i}}
for
i\in[1,n]
is defined in
[
25
]
. Each graph
G=[\odot_{\textrm{plan}}]^{n}_{i=1}H_{a_{i}b_{i}c_{i}d_{i}}
is a planar graph; see examples shown in Fig.
29
.
Find

\min\{|V(G)|:~G=[\odot_{\textrm{plan}}]^{n}_{i=1}H_{a_{i}b_{i}c_{i}d_{i}}\}
.

Figure 29:
Different planar graphs obtained by vertex-coinciding operation to the Hanzi-based string
H_{an}
shown in Fig.
27
.

Finding another planar graph
G^{*}
forms a semi-planar graph

G^{semi}=G^{*}[\odot_{\textrm{plan}}]G=G^{*}[\odot_{\textrm{plan}}]\Big([\odot_{\textrm{plan}}]^{n}_{i=1}H_{a_{i}b_{i}c_{i}d_{i}}\Big)

such that the vertex number
|V(G^{*})|
is as smaller as possible.

Conversely, a semi-planar graph
J
can be vertex-split into different groups of Hanzi-graphs
G_{i,1}
,
G_{i,2}
,
\dots
,
G_{i,n_{i}}
, and put them into a set
C_{split}(J)
,
determine
the set
C_{split}(J)
and the number
\min\{n_{i}\}
.

(ii)
Color
planar graphs
G=[\odot_{\textrm{plan}}]^{n}_{i=1}H_{a_{i}b_{i}c_{i}d_{i}}
based on a Hanzi-graph base
\textbf{{H}}_{an}
by the indexed-colorings defined in Definition
49
.

(iii) We have a
[\odot_{\textrm{plan}}]
-graphic lattice
defined as

\textbf{{L}}([\odot_{\textrm{plan}}]Z^{0}\textbf{{H}}_{an})=\Big\{[\odot_{\textrm{plan}}]^{n}_{i=1}\lambda_{i}H_{a_{i}b_{i}c_{i}d_{i}}:\lambda_{i}\in Z^{0},H_{a_{i}b_{i}c_{i}d_{i}}\in\textbf{{H}}_{an}\Big\}

(238)

based on the Hanzi-graph base
\textbf{{H}}_{an}
shown in Eq.(
237
), where
\sum^{n}_{i=1}\lambda_{i}\geq 1
.

A process of starting from a semi-planar graph
J
and coming back to a semi-planar graph
G^{semi}
is as follows:

J\rightarrow_{vsplt}\textbf{{H}}_{an}\rightarrow\textbf{{L}}([\odot_{\textrm{plan}}]Z^{0}\textbf{{H}}_{an})\rightarrow G\rightarrow G^{*}[\odot_{\textrm{plan}}]G=G^{semi}

(239)

5.8.2
Hanzi-Lattices

In
[
59
]
, the authors point out that graphic lattices not only have their important applications in information security and mathematics, but also have their potential in other fields. As known, there are 250 “Pianpang” and “Bushou” in Chinese characters (Hanzis) according to “Chinese Character GB18030-2000” containing 27484 Chinese characters, where “Pianpang” is the right and left part of a Chinese character, and “Bushou” can arrange Chinese characters; we have 299 “Dutizi”; and there are 16 punctuation being the marks to clarify meaning by indicating separation of words into sentences and clauses and phrases. Let
H_{an}(1),H_{an}(2),\dots,H_{an}(565)
indicate all of “Pianpang”, “Bushou”, “Dutizi” and punctuation in Chinese characters, so we call
\textbf{{H}}_{an}=(H_{an}(1),H_{an}(2),\dots,H_{an}(565))
a
Hanzi-Pianpang base
. Thereby, we can build up a
Hanzi-literary lattice

\textbf{{L}}(Z^{0}\textbf{{H}}_{an})
of various Chinese writings by

\textbf{{L}}(Z^{0}\textbf{{H}}_{an})=\left\{C_{para}=\bigcup^{565}_{k=1}x_{k}H_{an}(k):x_{k}\in Z^{0},H_{an}(k)\in\textbf{{H}}_{an}\right\}

(240)

with
\sum^{565}_{k=1}x_{k}\geq 1
. Let
C_{para}=\sum^{565}_{k=1}x_{k}H_{an}(k)
in
\textbf{{L}}(Z^{0}\textbf{{H}}_{an})
. We can confirm the following facts:

Problem 23
.

We need to consider the following problems:

Prob
-1.

List
all Chinese paragraphs in
\textbf{{L}}(Z^{0}\textbf{{H}}_{an})
with
\sum^{565}_{k=1}x_{k}\leq M
for a fixed integer
M\in Z^{0}\setminus\{0\}
.

Prob
-2.

Judge
whether each
C_{para}
defined in Eq.(
240
) is meaningful or meaningless in Chinese.

Considering the above Prob-1 and Prob-2, we can build another
Hanzi-literary lattice

\textbf{{L}}(Z^{0}\textbf{{C}}_{cha})=\left\{\bigcup^{27500}_{k=1}y_{k}C_{cha}(k):y_{k}\in Z^{0},C_{cha}(k)\in\textbf{{C}}_{cha}\right\}

(241)

based on a
Hanzi-graph base

\textbf{{C}}_{cha}=(C_{cha}(1),C_{cha}(2),\dots,C_{cha}(27500))
and
\sum^{27500}_{k=17}y_{k}\geq 1
, where each
C_{cha}(i)
with
i\in[1,16]
is a punctuation, and each
C_{cha}(j)
for
j\in[17,27500]
is a Chinese character in Chinese Character GB18030-2000.

Theorem 58
.

[
59
]
(1) Chinese paragraphs
C_{para}
defined in Eq.(
240
) can be used in text-based passwords to make more complicated
public-key graphs
and
private-key graphs
for increasing the cost of decryption and attackers.

(2) Each Chinese writing limited in Chinese Character GB18030-2000 has been contained in the Hanzi-literary lattices
\textbf{{L}}(Z^{0}\textbf{{H}}_{an})
defined in Eq.(
240
) and
\textbf{{L}}(Z^{0}\textbf{{C}}_{cha})
defined in Eq.(
241
), such as poems, novels, essay, proses, reports, news
et al.

Definition 50
.

[
40
]
Let
H=E^{*}+G
be a connected graph, where
E^{*}
is a non-empty set of edges and
G=\bigcup^{m}_{i=1}G_{i}
is a disconnected graph, where
G_{1},G_{2},\dots,G_{m}
are connected graphs and vertex-disjoint from each other. If
H
admits a (set-ordered) graceful labeling (resp. a (set-ordered) odd-graceful labeling)
f
, then we call
f
a
flawed (set-ordered) graceful labeling
(resp. a
flawed (set-ordered) odd-graceful labeling
) of
G
.
\square

Definition 51
.

[
40
]
Suppose that
G=\bigcup^{m}_{i=1}G_{i}
is a disconnected graph, where
G_{1},G_{2},\dots
,
G_{m}
are connected graphs and vertex-disjoint from each other. We have a connected graph
G+E^{*}
obtained by adding the edges of an edge set
E^{*}
to
G
. If
G+E^{*}
admits a
W
-constraint coloring
f:S\subseteq V(G)\cup E(G)\cup E^{*}\rightarrow[a,b]
, then we say that the disconnected graph
G
admits a
flawed
W
-constraint coloring

f:S\setminus E^{*}\rightarrow[a,b]
.
\square

.

[

]
If each Pianpang
H\,^{\prime}_{an}(k)
of a Hanzi-Pianpang base

(242)

admits a
flawed graceful labeling
.

5.8.3
Maximal planar graphs and Hanzi-based strings

Using maximal planar graphs and Hanzi-graphs of topological coding to make asymmetric topology ciphers is as follows:

(i) For a semi-maximal planar graph
G
as a
public-key graph
, its own adjacent matrix
A(G)
is unique under the matrix similarity operation, that is, if
B(G)
is an adjacent matrix of
G
, then there is a matrix
P
such that
B(G)=PA(G)P^{-1}
; and for another semi-maximal planar graph
H
as a
private-key graph
, its own adjacent matrix
A(H)
is unique under the matrix similarity operation in graph theory. Thus, these two semi-maximal planar graphs
G
and
H
can be distinguished in the computer.

(ii) There is a topological signature authentication
J=G[\overline{\ominus}^{cyc}_{k}]H
, since there is a common cycle
C_{k}
in two semi-maximal planar graphs
G
and
H
.

(iii) Two Topcode-matrices
T_{code}(G,f)
and
T_{code}(H,g)
of two semi-maximal planar graphs
G
and
H
may correspond to other two or more graphs. In other words, these two graphs
G
and
H
are not easy to be determined by the Topcode-matrices, since it will meet the Subgraph Isomorphic Problem. Fig.
31
shows us a topological signature authentication
J=G[\overline{\ominus}^{cyc}_{6}]H
that is a maximal planar graph admitting a coloring
F
induced by two colorings
f
and
g
.

(iv) We vertex-split the semi-maximal planar graph
H
(as a
private-key
) into mutually edge-disjoint graphs
H^{*},H_{1},H_{2},\dots,H_{k}
, such that each graph
H_{i}
for
i\in[1,k]
is a Hanzi-graph, and
H_{1}H_{2}\dots H_{k}
is a Hanzi-based string, but
H^{*}
is not a Hanzi-graph, sometimes, or
H^{*}
does not exist; refer to Fig.
30
.

(v) Recolor the topological signature authentication
J=G[\overline{\ominus}^{cyc}_{k}]H
by a new proper 4-coloring defined in Definition
49
, and induces number-based strings as desired.

Figure 30:
A semi-maximal planar graph
H=H_{\textrm{comb}}[\odot_{\textrm{plan}}]H^{*}
, where
H_{\textrm{comb}}
is obtained by doing the vertex-coinciding operation to the Hanzi-based string
H_{an}
shown in Fig.
27
.

Figure 31:
A topological signature authentication made by a maximal planar graph
J=G[\overline{\ominus}^{cyc}_{6}]H
, where
G
is a
public-key graph
, and the
private-key graph

H
is shown in Fig.
30
.

Problem 24
.

Vertex-splitting a semi-maximal planar graph
H
into a group of mutually edge-disjoint Hanzi-graphs
H_{1},H_{2},\dots,H_{k}
and a non-Hanzi-graph
H^{*}
is not a slight job, and there are many Hanzi-based strings
H_{i_{1}}H_{i_{2}}\dots H_{i_{k}}
obtained from the permutations of the mutually edge-disjoint Hanzi-graphs
H_{1},H_{2},\dots,H_{k}
; see Fig.
54
. We may obtain other group of edge-disjoint Hanzi-graphs
G_{1},G_{2},\dots,G_{m}
after vertex-splitting the semi-maximal planar graph
H
.

Thereby, we get a set
H_{vspl}(H)
of groups of mutually edge-disjoint Hanzi-graphs
H_{i,1},H_{i,2},\dots,H_{i,m_{i}}
after vertex-splitting the semi-maximal planar graph
H
, that is

H_{vspl}(H)=\Big\{V^{split}_{i}=\{H_{i,1},H_{i,2},\dots,H_{i,m_{i}}\}:i\in[1,n_{vspl}(H)]\Big\}

(243)

Determine
the set
H_{vspl}(H)
and the number
n_{vspl}(H)
.

5.8.4
The
[\odot_{\textrm{plan}}]
-Hanzi-graphic lattices

The number-based string
a_{j}b_{j}c_{j}d_{j}
of each Hanzi-graph
H_{a_{j}b_{j}c_{j}d_{j}}
of a
Hanzi-graph base

\textbf{{H}}_{GB18}=(H_{a_{1}b_{1}c_{1}d_{1}},H_{a_{2}b_{2}c_{2}d_{2}},\dots,H_{a_{27484}b_{27484}c_{27484}d_{27484}})

is defined in
[
25
]
, such that
H_{a_{i}b_{i}c_{i}d_{i}}\not\cong H_{a_{j}b_{j}c_{j}d_{j}}
if
i\neq j
.

For a permutation
G_{i_{1}},G_{i_{2}},\dots,G_{i_{M}}
of mutually disjoint Hanzi-graphs
\lambda_{j}H_{a_{j}b_{j}c_{j}d_{j}}
for
j\in[1,27484]
and
\lambda_{j}\in Z^{0}
, where
M=\sum^{27484}_{j=1}\lambda_{j}\geq 1
, we do the vertex-coinciding operation “
[\odot_{\textrm{plan}}]
” to the permutation
G_{i_{1}},G_{i_{2}},\dots,G_{i_{M}}
as:
T_{1}=G_{i_{1}}[\odot_{\textrm{plan}}]G_{i_{2}}
, where
T_{1}
is a planar graph, and then we get the second planar graph
T_{2}=T_{1}[\odot_{\textrm{plan}}]G_{i_{3}}
, go on in this way, we get planar graphs

T_{n+1}=T_{n}[\odot_{\textrm{plan}}]G_{i_{n+2}},~n\in[2,27482]

we write the last planar graph as

T_{27483}=T_{27482}[\odot_{\textrm{plan}}]G_{i_{27484}}=[\odot_{\textrm{plan}}]^{27484}_{j=1}\lambda_{j}H_{a_{j}b_{j}c_{j}d_{j}}

(244)

We get a
vertex-coincided Hanzi-graphic lattice

\textbf{{L}}([\odot_{\textrm{plan}}]Z^{0}\textbf{{H}}_{GB18})=\Big\{[\odot_{\textrm{plan}}]^{27484}_{j=1}\lambda_{j}H_{a_{j}b_{j}c_{j}d_{j}}:\lambda_{j}\in Z^{0},H_{a_{j}b_{j}c_{j}d_{j}}\in\textbf{{H}}_{GB18}\Big\}

(245)

based on the
Hanzi-graph base

\textbf{{H}}_{GB18}
.

Theorem 60
.

∗
Since each graph
G
of the Hanzi-graphic lattice
\textbf{{L}}([\odot_{\textrm{plan}}]Z^{0}\textbf{{H}}_{GB18})
defined in Eq.(
245
) is a planar graph, then the planar graph
G
admits a total indexed-coloring defined in Definition
49
and can be decomposed into mutually edge-disjoint Hanzi-graphs.

6
Number-based strings towards application

For encrypting the whole network and assigning passwords to each node of the network one time, we design several string groups, string lattices, graph homomorphisms based on the strings introduced in the previous sections.

6.1
Multi-level multi-rank strings

6.1.1
Definition

Definition 52
.

∗
An
m
-level
\{n_{i}\}^{m}_{i=1}
-rank string-set
contains its own elements being strings
s_{tri}(m)=A^{m}_{1}A^{m}_{2}\cdots A^{m}_{n_{m}}
, where

A^{m}_{j}=A^{m-1}_{j,1}A^{m-1}_{j,2}\cdots A^{m-1}_{j,n_{m-1}}
for
j\in[1,n_{m}]
, and

A^{m-s+1}_{j}=A^{m-s}_{j,1}A^{m-s}_{j,2}\cdots A^{m-s}_{j,n_{m-s}},~j\in[1,n_{m-s+1}],~s\in[1,m-1]

(246)

Finally, each
A^{2}_{j}=A^{1}_{j,1}A^{1}_{j,2}\cdots A^{1}_{j,n_{1}}
is a
[0,9]
-string with
A^{1}_{j,t}\in[0,9]
and
t\in[1,n_{1}]
and
j\in[1,n_{2}]
.

If
n_{i}=n
for
i\in[1,m]
, we call
s_{tri}(m)

m
-level uniformly
n
-rank string
.
\square

6.1.2
A new representation of Topcode-matrices

Suppose that a
(p,q)
-graph
G
admits a total string-coloring
h:V(G)\cup E(G)\rightarrow\textbf{{S}}_{tring}(n)
, such that

h(u_{k})=r_{k,1}r_{k,2}\cdots r_{k,n},~h(v_{k})=s_{k,1}s_{k,2}\cdots s_{k,n},~h(u_{k}v_{k})=t_{k,1}t_{k,2}\cdots t_{k,n}

(247)

for each edge
u_{k}v_{k}\in E(G)=\{u_{k}v_{k}:k\in[1,q]\}
. Then the
(p,q)
-graph
G
has its own Topcode-matrix

\centering{\begin{split}T_{code}(G,h)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}h(u_{1})&h(u_{2})&\cdots&h(u_{q})\\
h(u_{1}v_{1})&h(u_{2}v_{2})&\cdots&h(u_{q}v_{q})\\
h(v_{1})&h(v_{2})&\cdots&h(v_{q})\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccccccccccc}X_{h}\\
E_{h}\\
Y_{h}\end{array}\right)=(X_{h},E_{h},Y_{h})^{T}\end{split}}\@add@centering

(248)

where
u_{k}v_{k}\in E(G)
for
k\in[1,q]
.

We rewrite each group of colors
h(u_{k}),h(u_{k}v_{k})
and
h(v_{k})
into three vectors, immediately, we get the Topcode-matrix of each graph
G_{u_{k}v_{k}}
as follows:

\centering{\begin{split}T_{code}(G_{u_{k}v_{k}},h)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}r_{k,1}&r_{k,2}&\cdots&r_{k,n}\\
t_{k,1}&t_{k,2}&\cdots&t_{k,n}\\
s_{k,1}&s_{k,2}&\cdots&s_{k,n}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccccccccccc}h(u_{k})\\
h(u_{k}v_{k})\\
h(v_{k})\end{array}\right)=(h(u_{k}),h(u_{k}v_{k}),h(v_{k}))^{T}\end{split}}\@add@centering

(249)

with edge number
|E(G_{u_{k}v_{k}})|=n
for
k\in[1,q]
. We give the Topcode-matrix
T_{code}(G,h)
a new representation as:

T_{code}(G,h):=(T_{code}(G_{u_{1}v_{1}},h),T_{code}(G_{u_{2}v_{2}},h),\dots,T_{code}(G_{u_{q}v_{q}},h))

(250)

which differs from that defined in Definition
28
.

of

for

.

.

∗
It is not hard to verify that three multiple-edge graphs

6.2
Graph-colorings based on graphic group

6.2.1
Infinite graphic groups

INFINITEGraphic-group Algorithm

[
37
]
. Let
f:V(G)\cup E(G)\rightarrow[1,M]
be a
W
-constraint proper total coloring of a graph
G
such that two color sets
f(V(G))=\{f(x):x\in V(G)\}
and
f(E(G))=\{f(uv):uv\in E(G)\}
hold a collection of constraints true. We define a
W
-constraint proper total coloring
g_{s,k}
by setting

g_{s,k}(x)=f(x)+s~(\bmod~p),x\in V(G)

and

g_{s,k}(uv)=f(uv)+k~(\bmod~q),~uv\in E(G)

Let
F_{f}(G)
be the set of graphs
G_{s,k}
admitting
W
-constraint proper total colorings
g_{s,k}
defined above, and each graph
G_{s,k}\cong G
in topological structure. We define an additive operation “
[\oplus\ominus]
” on the elements of
F_{f}(G)
in the following way: Take arbitrarily an element
G_{a,b}\in F_{f}(G)
as
zero
, and

G_{s,k}[\oplus\ominus_{a,b}]G_{i,j}:=G_{s,k}[\oplus]G_{i,j}[\ominus]G_{a,b}

(253)

is defined by the following computation

[g_{s,k}(w)+g_{i,j}(w)-g_{a,b}(w)]~(\bmod~\varepsilon)=g_{\lambda,\mu}(w)

(254)

for each element
w\in V(G)\cup E(G)
, where the index
\lambda=s+i-a~(\bmod~p)
and the index
\mu=k+j-b~(\bmod~q)
. As
w=x\in V(G)
, the form (
254
) is just equal to

[g_{s,k}(x)+g_{i,j}(x)-g_{a,b}(x)]~(\bmod~p)=g_{\lambda,\mu}(x)

(255)

and as
w=uv\in E(G)
, the form (
254
) is defined as follows:

[g_{s,k}(uv)+g_{i,j}(uv)-g_{a,b}(uv)]~(\bmod~q)=g_{\lambda,\mu}(uv)

(256)

Especially, as
s=i=a=\alpha
, we have
\bmod~\varepsilon=\bmod~q
in Eq.(
254
), and

[g_{\alpha,k}(uv)+g_{\alpha,j}(uv)-g_{\alpha,b}(uv)]~(\bmod~q)=g_{\alpha,\mu}(uv)

(257)

for
uv\in E(G)
; and when
k=j=b=\beta
, so
\bmod~\varepsilon=\bmod~p
in Eq.(
254
), we have

[g_{s,\beta}(x)+g_{i,\beta}(x)-g_{a,\beta}(x)]~(\bmod~p)=g_{\lambda,\beta}(x),~x\in V(G)

(258)

Definition 53
.

[
37
]
Sice the graph set
F_{f}(G)
made by the INFINITEGraphic-group Algorithm holds:

(1)

Zero.
Each graph
G_{a,b}\in F_{f}(G)
can be determined as a
preappointed zero
such that

G_{s,k}[\oplus\ominus_{a,b}]G_{i,j}:=G_{s,k}[\oplus]G_{a,b}[\ominus]G_{a,b}=G_{s,k}

(2)

Uniqueness.
If

G_{s,k}[\oplus]G_{i,j}[\ominus]G_{a,b}=G_{c,d}\in F_{f}(G)\textrm{ and }G_{s,k}[\oplus]G_{i,j}[\ominus]G_{a,b}=G_{r,t}\in F_{f}(G)

then the index
c=s+i-a~(\bmod~p)=r
and the index
d=k+j-b~(\bmod~q)=t
under any
preappointed zero

G_{a,b}
.

(3)

Inverse.
Each graph
G_{s,k}\in F_{f}(G)
has its own
inverse

G_{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\in F_{f}(G)
such that

G_{s,k}[\oplus\ominus_{a,b}]G_{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}}:=G_{s,k}[\oplus]G_{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}}[\ominus]G_{a,b}=G_{a,b}

determined by
[g_{s,k}(w)+g_{i,j}(w)]~(\bmod~\varepsilon)=2g_{a,b}(w)
for each element
w\in V(G)\cup E(G)
.

(4)

Associative law.
Each triple
G_{s,k},G_{i,j},G_{c,d}\in F_{f}(G)
holds

G_{s,k}[\oplus]\big([G_{i,j}[\oplus]G_{c,d}[\ominus]G_{a,b}]\big)[\ominus]G_{a,b}=\big([G_{s,k}[\oplus]G_{i,j}[\ominus]G_{a,b}]\big)[\oplus]G_{c,d}[\ominus]G_{a,b}

true, that is

\big(G_{s,k}[\oplus\ominus_{a,b}]G_{i,j}\big)[\oplus\ominus_{a,b}]G_{c,d}=G_{s,k}[\oplus\ominus_{a,b}]\big(G_{i,j}[\oplus\ominus_{a,b}]G_{c,d}\big)

(5)

Commutative law.

G_{s,k}[\oplus]G_{i,j}[\ominus]G_{a,b}=G_{i,j}[\oplus]G_{s,k}[\ominus]G_{a,b}
, also

G_{s,k}[\oplus\ominus_{a,b}]G_{i,j}=G_{i,j}[\oplus\ominus_{a,b}]G_{s,k}

Thereby, we call
F_{f}(G)=\{G_{s,k}:s\in[0,p],k\in[0,q]\}
an
every-zero graphic group
based on the additive operation “
[\oplus\ominus]
” defined in Eq.(
254
), and write this group by
\textbf{{G}}=\{F_{f}(G);\oplus\ominus\}
.

There are
pq
graphs in the every-zero graphic group
G
. There are two particular
every-zero graphic subgroups

\{F_{v}(G);\oplus\ominus\}\subset\textbf{{G}}
and
\{F_{e}(G);\oplus\ominus\}\subset\textbf{{G}}
, where
F_{v}(G)=\{G_{s,0}:s\in[0,p]\}
and
F_{e}(G)=\{G_{0,k}:k\in[0,q]\}
. In fact,
G
contains at least
(p+q)
different every-zero graphic subgroups.
\square

Definition 54
.

[
56
]
For two every-zero graphic groups
\{F_{f}(G);\oplus\ominus\}
based on a graph set
F_{f}(G)=\{G_{i}\}^{m}_{1}
and
\{F_{h}(H);\oplus\ominus\}
based on a graph set
F_{h}(H)=\{H_{i}\}^{m}_{1}
, suppose that there are graph homomorphisms
G_{i}\rightarrow H_{i}
defined by
\theta_{i}:V(G_{i})\rightarrow V(H_{i})
with
i\in[1,m]
. We define
\theta=\bigcup^{m}_{i=1}\theta_{i}
, and have an
every-zero graphic group homomorphism

\{F_{f}(G);\oplus\ominus\}\rightarrow\{F_{h}(H);\oplus\ominus\}

from a graph set
F_{f}(G)
to another graph set
F_{h}(H)
.
\square

Infinite graphic-sequence groups.
Infinite graphic-sequence groups have been introduced in
[
57
]
. Suppose that a connected
(p,q)
-graph
G
admits a
W
-constraint total coloring
f
, we define
W
-constraint total colorings
f_{s,k}
by setting
f_{s,k}(x)=f(x)+s
for every vertex
x\in V(G)
, and
f_{s,k}(uv)=f(uv)+k
for each edge
uv\in E(G)
as two integers
s,k
belong to the set
Z
of integers. So, we have each connected
(p,q)
-graph
G_{s,k}\cong G
admits a
W
-constraint total coloring
f_{s,k}
defined above, immediately, we get an infinite graphic-sequence
\{\{G_{s,k}\}^{+\infty}_{-\infty}\}^{+\infty}_{-\infty}
. We take a graph
G_{a,b}\in\{\{G_{s,k}\}^{+\infty}_{-\infty}\}^{+\infty}_{-\infty}
as a
preappointed zero
and any two
G_{s,k}
and
G_{i,j}
in
\{\{G_{s,k}\}^{+\infty}_{-\infty}\}^{+\infty}_{-\infty}
, and do the additive computation “
G_{s,k}[\oplus\ominus_{a,b}]G_{i,j}:=G_{s,k}[\oplus]G_{i,j}[\ominus]G_{a,b}
” in the following way: For each edge
uv\in E(G)
,

[f_{s,k}(uv)+f_{i,j}(uv)-f_{a,b}(uv)]~(\bmod~q_{W})=f_{\lambda,\mu}(uv).

(259)

with the index
\mu=k+j-b~(\bmod~q_{W})
; and for each vertex
x\in V(G)
,

[f_{s,k}(x)+f_{i,j}(x)-f_{a,b}(x)]~(\bmod~p_{W})=f_{\lambda,\mu}(x)

(260)

with the index
\lambda=s+i-a~(\bmod~p_{W})
.

Here,
p_{W}=|V(G)|
and
q_{W}=|E(G)|
if the
W
-constraint total coloring
f
is a gracefully total coloring; and
p_{W}=q_{W}=2|E(G)|
if the
W
-constraint total coloring
f
is an odd-gracefully total coloring (see examples shown in Fig.
34
and Fig.
35
).

Figure 34:

An example for
p_{W}=2|E(G)|=10
and holding Eq.(
260
), cited from
[
49
]
.

Figure 35:

An example for
q_{W}=2|E(G)|=10
and holding Eq.(
259
), cited from
[
49
]
.

Especially, for an edge subsequence
G_{s,k},G_{s,k+1},\dots,G_{s,k+q_{W}}
, and a vertex subsequence
G_{s,k}
,
G_{s+1,k}
,
\dots
,
G_{s+p_{W},k}
, we have two sets
F(\{G_{s,k+j}\}^{q_{W}}_{j=1};\oplus\ominus;(G,f))
and
F(\{G_{s+i,k}\}^{p_{W}}_{i=1};\oplus\ominus;(G,f))
. By the operation “
[\oplus\ominus]
” defined in Eq.(
259
), we claim that
F(\{G_{s,k+j}\}^{q_{W}}_{j=1};\oplus\ominus;(G,f))
is an
every-zero edge-graphic group
, since there are the following facts:

(i)
Zero
. Every graph
G_{s,k+j}
of
F(\{G_{s,k+j}\}^{q}_{j=1};\oplus\ominus)
is as a
preappointed zero
such that

G_{s,k+r}[\oplus\ominus_{s,k+j}]G_{s,k+j}:=G_{s,k+r}[\oplus]G_{s,k+j}[\ominus]G_{s,k+j}=G_{s,k+r}

for any graph
G_{s,k+r}\in F(\{G_{s,k+j}\}^{q}_{j=1};\oplus\ominus)
.

(ii)
Closure law
. For the index
r=i+j-j_{0}~(\bmod~q_{W})
,

G_{s,k+r}[\oplus\ominus_{s,k+j_{0}}]G_{s,k+j}:=G_{s,k+i}[\oplus]G_{s,k+j}[\ominus]G_{s,k+j_{0}}=G_{s,k+r}\in F(\{G_{s,k+j}\}^{q}_{j=1};\oplus\ominus)

under any
preappointed zero

G_{s,k+j_{0}}
, and
G_{s,k+r},G_{s,k+j}\in F(\{G_{s,k+j}\}^{q}_{j=1};\oplus\ominus)
.

(iii)
Inverse.
For the index
i+j=2j_{0}~(\bmod~q_{W})
, we have

G_{s,k+i}[\oplus\ominus_{s,k+j_{0}}]G_{s,k+j}:=G_{s,k+i}[\oplus]G_{s,k+j}[\ominus]G_{s,k+j}=G_{s,k+j_{0}}

under any
preappointed zero

G_{s,k+j_{0}}
, and
G_{s,k+i},G_{s,k+j}\in F(\{G_{s,k+j}\}^{q}_{j=1};\oplus\ominus)
.

(iv)
Associative law
. We have

G_{s,k+i}[\oplus\ominus_{s,k+j_{0}}]\big(G_{s,k+j}[\oplus\ominus_{s,k+j_{0}}]G_{s,k+r}\big)=\big(G_{s,k+i}[\oplus\ominus_{s,k+j_{0}}]G_{s,k+j}\big)[\oplus\ominus_{s,k+j_{0}}]G_{s,k+r}

under any
preappointed zero

G_{s,k+j_{0}}
, and
G_{s,k+i},G_{s,k+j},G_{s,k+r}\in F(\{G_{s,k+j}\}^{q}_{j=1};\oplus\ominus)
.

(v)
Commutative law
. For any
preappointed zero

G_{s,k+j_{0}}
, there is

G_{s,k+i}[\oplus\ominus_{s,k+j_{0}}]G_{s,k+j}=G_{s,k+j}[\oplus\ominus_{s,k+j_{0}}]G_{s,k+i}

for
G_{s,k+i},G_{s,k+j}\in F(\{G_{s,k+j}\}^{q}_{j=1};\oplus\ominus)
.

Similarly,
F(\{G_{s+i,k}\}^{p_{W}}_{i=1};\oplus\ominus;(G,f))
is an
every-zero vertex-graphic group
by the operation “
[\oplus\ominus]
” defined in Eq.(
260
).

As considering some graphs arbitrarily selected from the sequence
\{\{G_{s,k}\}^{+\infty}_{-\infty}\}^{+\infty}_{-\infty}
, we have

[f_{s,k}(w)+f_{i,j}(w)-f_{a,b}(w)]~(\bmod~p_{W},q_{W})=f_{\lambda,\mu}(w).

(261)

with the indices
\lambda=s+i-a~(\bmod~p_{W})
and
\mu=k+j-b~(\bmod~q_{W})
for each element
w\in V(G)\cup E(G)
. See examples shown in Fig.
36
and Fig.
37
.

Thereby, we call the set
F(\{\{G_{s,k}\}^{+\infty}_{-\infty}\}^{+\infty}_{-\infty};\oplus\ominus;(G,f))
an
every-zero infinite graphic-sequence group
under the additive operation “
[\oplus\ominus]
” based on two modules
p_{W}
and
q_{W}
and a connected
(p,q)
-graph
G
admitting a
W
-constraint total coloring, since it possesses the properties of Zero, Closure law, Inverse, Associative law and Commutative law.

Figure 36:

A graphic scheme for illustrating the formula Eq.(
261
), cited from
[
49
]
.

Figure 37:

Two examples for illustrating the formula Eq.(
261
), cited from
[
49
]
.

Remark 38
.

[
49
]
Let
F^{*}(G,f)=F(\{\{G_{s,k}\}^{+\infty}_{-\infty}\}^{+\infty}_{-\infty};\oplus\ominus;(G,f))
be an every-zero infinite graphic-sequence group. The elements of the every-zero infinite graphic-sequence group
F^{*}(G,f)
can tile fully each point
(x,y)
of
xOy
-plane. And moreover,
F^{*}(G,f)
contains infinite every-zero graphic groups having finite elements, such as
F(\{G_{s+i,k}\}^{p_{W}}_{i=1}
;
[\oplus\ominus];(G,f))
and
F(\{G_{s,k+j}\}^{q_{W}}_{i=1};\oplus\ominus;(G,f))
. Also,
F^{*}(G,f)
contains infinite every-zero graphic groups of infinite elements.

Clearly, particular every-zero graphic groups having infinite elements or finite elements can be used easily to encrypt randomly the nodes of networks. Suppose that the coloring
f
of
G
in
F^{*}(G,f)
is equivalent to another
W_{g}
-constraint total coloring
g
of
G
. Then we get another every-zero infinite graphic-sequence group
F^{*}(G,g)=F(\{\{G_{i,j}\}^{+\infty}_{-\infty}\}^{+\infty}_{-\infty};\oplus\ominus;(G,g))
with
G\cong G_{i,j}
. Thereby, the every-zero infinite graphic-sequence group
F^{*}(G,f)
is a
public-key graphic-sequence group
, the every-zero infinite graphic-sequence group
F^{*}(G,g)
is a
private-key graphic-sequence group
accordingly.

Since there exists a mapping
\varphi:V(G)\cup E(G)\rightarrow V(G)\cup E(G)
such that
g(w)=\varphi(f(w))
for
w\in V(G)\cup E(G)
, we claim that
F^{*}(G,f)
admits an every-zero graphic-sequence homomorphism to
F^{*}(G,g)
, and moreover

F^{*}(G,f)\leftrightarrow F^{*}(G,g),

(262)

is a pair of
homomorphically equivalent every-zero graphic-sequence homomorphisms
.
\parallel

6.2.2
Graphic groups in encrypting dynamic networks

In
[
49
]
, the author have designed the
every-zero infinite graphic groups
with no modular: For a bipartite connected
(p,q)
-graph
H
admitting a set-ordered
W_{g}
-constraint total coloring
g
such that
\max g(X)<\min g(Y)
for the bipartition
(X,Y)
of vertices of
H
, we define a graph
H_{i,j}
holding
H_{i,j}\cong H
and admitting a
W_{g}
-constraint total coloring
h_{i,j}
defined as: Let
i,j\in Z
,
h_{i,j}(u)=g(u)+i
for each vertex
u\in X
, and
h_{i,j}(v)=g(v)+j
for each vertex
v\in Y
, and
h_{i,j}(uv)=g(uv)+i+j
for each edge
uv\in E(H)
. Next, the operation “
H_{i,j}[\oplus\ominus_{a,b}]H_{k,s}:=H_{i,j}[\oplus]H_{k,s}[\ominus]H_{a,b}
” is defined in the following: For a fixed
H_{a,b}
admitting a
W_{g}
-constraint total coloring
h_{a,b}
, we set

h_{i,j}(u)+h_{k,s}(u)-h_{a,b}(u)=h_{\lambda,\mu}(u)+\lambda,~u\in X

(263)

with the index
\lambda=i+k-a
, and

h_{i,j}(v)+h_{k,s}(v)-h_{a,b}(v)=h_{\lambda,\mu}(v)+\mu,~v\in Y

(264)

with the index
\mu=j+s-b
, and for
uv\in E(H)
, thus, we have

{\begin{split}h_{i,j}(uv)+h_{k,s}(uv)-h_{a,b}(uv)=h_{\pi,\tau}(uv)+\pi+\tau\end{split}}

(265)

with the indices
\pi=i+k-a
and
\tau=j+s-b
.

Thereby, we get an infinite graph set
(\textbf{{H}}_{total})^{+\infty}_{-\infty}|^{2}=\{H_{s,k}:s,k\in Z\}
, which is an
every-zero infinite graphic group
based on Eq.(
263
), Eq.(
264
) and Eq.(
265
), as well as

2h_{a,b}(u)+h_{i,j}(u)=h_{2a+i,2b+j}(u)=g(u)+2a+i
for
u\in X
,

2h_{a,b}(v)+h_{i,j}(v)=h_{2a+i,2b+j}(v)=g(v)+2b+j
for
v\in Y
and

2h_{a,b}(uv)+h_{i,j}(uv)=h_{2a+i,2b+j}(uv)=g(uv)+2(a+b)+i+j
for
uv\in E(H)
.

MULTIPLE-JOIN algorithm
is based on an every-zero infinite graphic group
(\textbf{{H}}_{total})^{+\infty}_{-\infty}|^{2}
.

Initialization.
Select arbitrarily an element
H_{s_{0},k_{0}}\in(\textbf{{H}}_{total})^{+\infty}_{-\infty}|^{2}
; two ends
i
and
j
of each edge
ij
of the network model
N(0)
are encrypted by
H_{s_{i},k_{i}}
and
H_{s_{j},k_{j}}
and the edge
ij
is encrypted by
h_{\pi,\tau}(ij)+\pi+\tau
with
\pi=s_{i}+s_{j}-s_{0}
and
\tau=k_{i}+k_{j}-k_{0}
. We call
H_{s_{0},k_{0}}
an
encryption base
of the network
N(0)
.

Iteration.
Assume that a network model
N(t)
is encrypted well by the every-zero infinite graphic group
(\textbf{{H}}_{total})^{+\infty}_{-\infty}|^{2}
with the encryption base
H_{s_{t},k_{t}}
. Add a new vertex
u
to the network
N(t)
, and join it with vertices
x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{d}
of the network model
N(t)
for produce a new network model
N(t+1)
, where each
x_{r}
was encrypted by
H_{s_{x_{r}},k_{x_{r}}}
with
r\in[1,d]
; take arbitrarily an element
H_{s_{t+1},k_{t+1}}
as an encryption base of the network model
N(t+1)
and encrypt
u
by selecting randomly
H_{s_{u},k_{u}}\in(\textbf{{H}}_{total})^{+\infty}_{-\infty}|^{2}
, each edge
ux_{r}
is encrypted by
h_{\pi,\tau}(ux_{r})+\pi+\tau
with the indices
\pi=s_{u}+s_{x_{r}}-s_{t+1}
and
\tau=k_{u}+k_{x_{r}}-k_{t+1}
.

Since, in the MULTIPLE-JOIN algorithm, the vertices and edges of the network model
N(t)
are encrypted by the graphs of
(\textbf{{H}}_{total})^{+\infty}_{-\infty}|^{2}
, we construct another network model
R(t)
by replacing each vertex of
N(t)
with the graphs of
(\textbf{{H}}_{total})^{+\infty}_{-\infty}|^{2}
, each edge
uv
of
N(t)
is replaced by
H_{s_{uv},k_{uv}}=H_{s_{u},k_{u}}[\oplus]H_{s_{v},k_{v}}[\ominus]H_{a,b}
, and use new edges to join
H_{s_{u},k_{u}}
with
H_{s_{uv},k_{uv}}
together, and to join
H_{s_{uv},k_{uv}}
with
H_{s_{v},k_{v}}
together. We write

R(t)=N(t)\big[\overline{\odot}_{uv\in E(N(t))}\big]H_{s_{uv},k_{uv}}\langle H_{s_{u},k_{u}},H_{s_{v},k_{v}}\rangle,

and moreover we get an
infinite graphic group lattice

{\begin{split}\textbf{{L}}(\textbf{{F}}\overline{\odot}\textbf{{H}}_{total})=\big\{R(t):N(t)\in\textbf{{F}}(n_{v},n_{e})(t);H_{s_{uv},k_{uv}},H_{s_{u},k_{u}},H_{s_{v},k_{v}}\in(\textbf{{H}}_{total})^{+\infty}_{-\infty}|^{2}\big\}\end{split}}

(266)

under the infinite base
(\textbf{{H}}_{total})^{+\infty}_{-\infty}|^{2}
, where
\textbf{{F}}(n_{v},n_{e})(t)
is a set of networks having vertex numbers
\leq n_{v}(t)
and edge numbers
\leq n_{e}(t)
.

Remark 39
.

The MULTIPLE-JOIN algorithm has the following advantages:

Advantage
-1.

In the process of forming
N(t+1)
, it is allowed that a new vertex joins
d~(\geq 2)
vertices of
N(t)
, so it is easy to produce particular network models to meet the needs of the
growth
and the
preferential attachment
of BA-models
[
7
]
, and we have
n_{v}(t+1)=n_{v}(t)+1
and
n_{e}(t+1)=n_{e}(t)+d
.

Advantage
-2.

It is allowed to
add new edges
to joining vertices of
N(t)
, and
remove old edges
of
N(t)
in the process of forming
N(t+1)
for approximating real networks.

Advantage
-3.

Since an every-zero infinite graphic group
(\textbf{{H}}_{total})^{+\infty}_{-\infty}|^{2}
contains infinite elements, and
N(t_{i})
and
N(t_{j})
have different encryption bases at two distinct time steps
t_{i},t_{j}
, so it increases the cost of deciphering
N(t)
.

Advantage
-4.

Suppose that each graph
H_{s,k}
admits a graph homomorphism to a graph
G_{s,k}
, that is
H_{s,k}\rightarrow G_{s,k}
, then we can get another a graph homomorphism group
(\textbf{{G}}_{total})^{+\infty}_{-\infty}|^{2}=\{G_{s,k}:s,k\in Z\}
, so we get an
every-zero infinite graphic group homomorphism

(\textbf{{H}}_{total})^{+\infty}_{-\infty}|^{2}\rightarrow(\textbf{{G}}_{total})^{+\infty}_{-\infty}|^{2}
.

Advantage
-5.

In
(\textbf{{H}}_{total})^{+\infty}_{-\infty}|^{2}
, if the bipartite graph
H
is a tree admitting a set-ordered graceful coloring
g
, then there are many colorings equivalent with
g
proven in
[
10
]
, so we get infinite graphs
T^{r}_{s,k}
admitting total coloring
\theta^{r}_{i,j}
equivalent with
h_{i,j}
defined in
(\textbf{{H}}_{total})^{+\infty}_{-\infty}|^{2}
. Hence, we have many every-zero infinite graphic groups
(\textbf{{T}}^{r}_{total})^{+\infty}_{-\infty}|^{2}=\{T^{r}_{s,k}:s,k\in Z\}
with
r\geq 1
. We write this mutually equivalent every-zero infinite graphic groups by
(\textbf{{H}}_{total})^{+\infty}_{-\infty}|^{2}~\sim~(\textbf{{T}}^{r}_{total})^{+\infty}_{-\infty}|^{2}
.

6.2.3
Multi-level graphic group graph-coloring algorithm

Using a graphic group colors a network overall, where each graph in the graphic group is also colored by another graphic group, we call this process
multi-level graphic group graph-coloring algorithm
(MLGGG-coloring algorithm).

Multi-level graphic group graph-coloring algorithm.

Initialization.
Let
G_{m}=(G_{m,1},G_{m,2},\dots,G_{m,n_{m}})
be a
graph base
with
G_{m,i}\not\cong G_{m,j}
if
i\neq j
. A connected graph
H
admits a proper total graph-coloring
\varphi:V(H)\cup E(H)\rightarrow\{F(G_{m});\oplus\ominus\}
, where
\{F(G_{m});\oplus\ominus\}
is an
every-zero graphic group
, such that

\varphi(xy):=\varphi(x)[\oplus\ominus_{p}]\varphi(y)

for a preappointed
zero

G_{m,p}\in G_{m}
, where
\varphi(x)=G_{m,i}\in G_{m}
,
\varphi(y)=G_{m,j}\in G_{m}
and
\varphi(xy)=G_{m,\lambda}\in G_{m}
with the index
\lambda=i+j-p~(\bmod~n_{m})
. We get a graphic Topcode-matrix

\centering{\begin{split}T_{code}(H,\varphi)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\varphi(x_{1})&\varphi(x_{2})&\cdots&\varphi(x_{q})\\
\varphi(x_{1}y_{1})&\varphi(x_{2}y_{2})&\cdots&\varphi(x_{q}y_{q})\\
\varphi(y_{1})&\varphi(y_{2})&\cdots&\varphi(y_{q})\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccccccccccc}X_{\varphi}\\
E_{\varphi}\\
Y_{\varphi}\end{array}\right)=(X_{\varphi},E_{\varphi},Y_{\varphi})^{T}\end{split}}\@add@centering

(267)

where each edge
x_{i}y_{i}\in E(H)
; refer to Definition
28
.

Step 1.
Each graph
G_{m,j}
for
j\in[1,n_{m}]
in the graph base
G_{m}
admits a proper total graph-coloring

\varphi_{m,j}:V(G_{m,j})\cup E(G_{m,j})\rightarrow\{F(G_{m-1});\oplus\ominus\}

based on a graph base
G_{m-1}=(G_{m-1,1},G_{m-1,2},\dots,G_{m-1,n_{m-1}})
.

Step
k
.
In each graph base
G_{m-k}=(G_{m-k,1},G_{m-k,2},\dots,G_{m-k,n_{m-k}})
, each graph
G_{m-k,j}
for
j\in[1,n_{m-k}]
admits a proper total graph-coloring

\varphi_{m-k,j}:V(G_{m-k,j})\cup E(G_{m-k,j})\rightarrow\{F(G_{m-k-1});\oplus\ominus\}

based on a graph base
G_{m-k-1}=(G_{m-k-1,1},G_{m-k-1,2},\dots,G_{m-k-1,n_{m-k-1}})
for
k\in[0,m-1]
.

Step
m-1
.
Each graph
G_{1,j}
for
j\in[1,n_{1}]
in the graph base
G_{1}=(G_{1,1},G_{1,2},\dots,G_{1,n_{1}})
admits a proper total graph-coloring

\varphi_{1,j}:V(G_{1,j})\cup E(G_{1,j})\rightarrow\{F(G_{0});\oplus\ominus\}

based on a graph base
G_{0}=(G_{0,1},G_{0,2},\dots,G_{0,n_{0}})
.

Step
m
.
Finally, each graph
G_{0,j}
for
j\in[1,n_{0}]
in the graph base
G_{0}
admits a proper total string-coloring

\theta_{0,j}:V(G_{0,j})\cup E(G_{0,j})\rightarrow S_{[0,9]\textrm{-string}}

where the
[0,9]
-string set
S_{[0,9]\textrm{-string}}=\{s_{i}=c_{i,1}c_{i,2}\cdots c_{i,n}:c_{i,j}\in[0,9]\}
.

Remark 40
.

The above every-zero graphic groups

\{F(G_{m});\oplus\ominus\},\{F(G_{m-1});\oplus\ominus\},\dots,\{F(G_{1});\oplus\ominus\},\{F(G_{0});\oplus\ominus\}

and a
[0,9]
-string set
S_{[0,9]\textrm{-string}}
form a multiple protection of a network, and it greatly improves the security of the whole network encryption. In the era of quantum computer, the time consumed by the multiple encryption mentioned here can be completely ignored.
\parallel

6.3
Vector-colorings made by strings

Since a number-based string
s=a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n}
can correspond to a vector
(a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{n})
, or a set
\{a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{n}\}
(no order), or a vertex-degree
\textrm{deg}=(a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{n})
(no order), we consider
vector groups
based on the following
vector set

V^{n}_{ector}\big(\{(9)_{j}\}^{n}_{j=1}\big)=\big\{v_{ector}(\{m_{j}\}^{n}_{j=1})=(C_{1,m_{1}},C_{2,m_{2}},\dots,C_{n,m_{n}}):m_{j}\in[1,(9)_{j}],j\in[1,n]\big\}

(268)

with notations
C_{1,m_{1}},C_{2,m_{2}},\dots,C_{n,m_{n}}
and
(9)_{j}
defined in Definition
15
; and consider
vertex-degree groups
based on the following
vertex-degree set

D^{n}_{egree}\big(\{(9)_{j}\}^{n}_{j=1}\big)=\big\{\textrm{deg}(\{m_{j}\}^{n}_{j=1})=(C_{1,m_{1}},C_{2,m_{2}},\dots,C_{n,m_{n}}):m_{j}\in[1,(9)_{j}],j\in[1,n]\big\}

(269)

with notations
C_{1,m_{1}},C_{2,m_{2}},\dots,C_{n,m_{n}}
and
(9)_{j}
defined in Definition

.

Let

(272)

for each edge

.

Definition 55
.

∗

Homogeneous
(abc)
-magic vector-colorings.

Example 32
.

In Definition
55
, a
W
-constraint total vector-coloring
\overrightarrow{\pi}:V(G)\cup E(G)\rightarrow\textbf{{V}}_{ector}(n)
holds one
W
-constraint of the following vector-operation constraints

\overrightarrow{\pi}(u_{k})+\overrightarrow{\pi}(v_{k})=\overrightarrow{\pi}(u_{k}v_{k}),~\overrightarrow{\pi}(u_{k}v_{k})+\overrightarrow{\pi}(v_{k})=\overrightarrow{\pi}(u_{k}),~\overrightarrow{\pi}(u_{k})+\overrightarrow{\pi}(u_{k}v_{k})=\overrightarrow{\pi}(v_{k})

or
\overrightarrow{\pi}(u_{k}v_{k})=\overrightarrow{\pi}(u_{k})\cdot\overrightarrow{\pi}(v_{k})
, or
\overrightarrow{\pi}(u_{k}v_{k})=\overrightarrow{\pi}(u_{k})\times\overrightarrow{\pi}(v_{k})
, or
\lambda=[\overrightarrow{\pi}(u_{k})\times\overrightarrow{\pi}(v_{k})]\cdot\overrightarrow{\pi}(u_{k}v_{k})
for each edge
u_{k}v_{k}\in E(G)=\{u_{k}v_{k}:k\in[1,q]\}
.
\square

Proposition 61
.

∗
For a fixed vector
\overrightarrow{\lambda}
, there are more groups of different vectors
\overrightarrow{a},\overrightarrow{b},\overrightarrow{c}
hold

(i) The
vector-edge-magic constraint

\overrightarrow{a}+\overrightarrow{b}+\overrightarrow{c}=\overrightarrow{\lambda}
.

(ii) The
vector-edge-difference constraint

\overrightarrow{c}+\overrightarrow{a}-\overrightarrow{b}=\overrightarrow{\lambda}
.

(iii) The
vector-felicitous-difference constraint

\overrightarrow{a}+\overrightarrow{b}-\overrightarrow{c}=\overrightarrow{\lambda}
, or
\overrightarrow{c}-(\overrightarrow{a}+\overrightarrow{b})=\overrightarrow{\lambda}
.

(iv) The
vector-graceful-difference constraint

(\overrightarrow{a}-\overrightarrow{b})-\overrightarrow{c}=\overrightarrow{\lambda}
, or
\overrightarrow{c}-(\overrightarrow{a}-\overrightarrow{b})=\overrightarrow{\lambda}
.

Definition 56
.

∗

Non-homogeneous magic vector-colorings.
Let
V_{ector}
be a set of vectors. Suppose that a graph
G
admits a total vector-coloring
F:V(G)\cup E(G)\rightarrow V_{ector}
, such that
F(u)=\overrightarrow{v}_{u}
,
F(v)=\overrightarrow{v}_{v}
, and
F(uv)=\overrightarrow{v}_{uv}
for each edge
uv\in E(G)
.

(i) If there is a fixed vector
\overrightarrow{\lambda}
, such that each edge
uv\in E(G)
holds the
vector-edge-magic constraint

\overrightarrow{v}_{u}+\overrightarrow{v}_{v}+\overrightarrow{v}_{uv}=\overrightarrow{\lambda}
true, we call
F

edge-magic total vector-coloring
.

(ii) If there is a fixed vector
\overrightarrow{\lambda}
, such that each edge
uv\in E(G)
holds one of
vector-edge-difference constraints

\overrightarrow{v}_{uv}+(\overrightarrow{v}_{u}-\overrightarrow{v}_{v})=\overrightarrow{\lambda}
and
\overrightarrow{v}_{uv}+(\overrightarrow{v}_{v}-\overrightarrow{v}_{u})=\overrightarrow{\lambda}
true, we call
F

edge-difference total vector-coloring
.

(iii) If there is a fixed vector
\overrightarrow{\lambda}
, such that each edge
uv\in E(G)
holds one of
vector-felicitous-difference constraints

\overrightarrow{v}_{uv}-(\overrightarrow{v}_{u}+\overrightarrow{v}_{v})=\overrightarrow{\lambda}
and
(\overrightarrow{v}_{v}+\overrightarrow{v}_{u})-\overrightarrow{v}_{uv}=\overrightarrow{\lambda}
true, we call
F

felicitous-difference total vector-coloring
.

(iv) If there is a fixed vector
\overrightarrow{\lambda}
, such that each edge
uv\in E(G)
holds one of
vector-graceful-difference constraints

(\overrightarrow{v}_{u}-\overrightarrow{v}_{v})-\overrightarrow{v}_{uv}=\overrightarrow{\lambda}
,
(\overrightarrow{v}_{v}-\overrightarrow{v}_{u})-\overrightarrow{v}_{uv}=\overrightarrow{\lambda}
,
\overrightarrow{v}_{uv}-(\overrightarrow{v}_{u}-\overrightarrow{v}_{v})=\overrightarrow{\lambda}
and
\overrightarrow{v}_{uv}-(\overrightarrow{v}_{v}-\overrightarrow{v}_{u})=\overrightarrow{\lambda}
true, we call
F

graceful-difference total vector-coloring
.
\square

Remark 43
.

{\begin{split}&\psi(u_{k})=\{a_{k,1},a_{k,2},\dots,a_{k,n(k,r)}\},~\psi(v_{k})=\{b_{k,1},b_{k,2},\dots,b_{k,n(k,s)}\},\\

(284)

for each edge

.

.

Proposition 62
.

∗
For a fixed set
U
, there are more groups of different sets
S_{a},S_{b},S_{c}
hold

(i) The
set-edge-magic constraint

S_{a}\cup S_{b}\cup S_{c}=U
.

(ii) The
set-edge-difference constraint

S_{c}\cup(S_{a}\setminus S_{b})=U
.

(iii) The
set-felicitous-difference constraint

(S_{a}\cup S_{b})\setminus S_{c}=U
.

(iv) The
set-graceful-difference constraint

(S_{a}\setminus S_{b})\setminus S_{c}=U
.

Definition 59
.

∗
Let
S_{set}
be a set of sets. Suppose that a graph
G
admits a total set-coloring
F:V(G)\cup E(G)\rightarrow S_{set}
, such that
F(u)=S_{u}
,
F(v)=S_{v}
, and
F(uv)=S_{uv}
for each edge
uv\in E(G)
.

(i) If there is a fixed set
U
, such that each edge
uv\in E(G)
holds the
set-edge-magic constraint

S_{u}\cup S_{v}\cup S_{uv}=U
true, we say
F

set-edge-magic total set-coloring
.

(ii) If there is a fixed set
U
, such that each edge
uv\in E(G)
holds one of
set-edge-difference constraints

S_{uv}\cup(S_{u}\setminus S_{v})=U
and
S_{uv}\cup(S_{v}\setminus S_{u})=U

F

set-edge-difference total set-coloring
.

(iii) If there is a fixed set
U
, such that each edge

(S_{u}\cup S_{v})\setminus S_{uv}=U
and
S_{uv}\setminus(S_{v}\cup S_{u})=U
true, we say
F

.

(iv) If there is a fixed set

, such that each edge

holds one of

(S_{u}\setminus S_{v})\setminus S_{uv}=U
,
(S_{v}\setminus S_{u})\setminus S_{uv}=U

and
S_{uv}\setminus(S_{v}\setminus S_{u})=U

.
\square

Theorem 63
.

∗
Each connected
(p,q)
-graph
G
admits a
proper total string-coloring

f:V(G)\cup E(G)\rightarrow\{a_{i}b_{i}:~a_{i},b_{i}\in[1,\Delta(G)-k]\}

with
k\leq\Delta(G)-\sqrt{3[1+\Delta(G)]}
if
\Delta(G)\geq 6
.

Proof.

As known, the chromatic index
\chi\,^{\prime}(G)\leq\Delta(G)+1
(Vadim G. Vizing, 1964) and the chromatic number
\chi(G)\leq\Delta(G)+1+K(G)
(Bruce Reed, 1998). Let
M=\Delta(G)-k
, so we have
M(M-1)+M=M^{2}
different number-based strings
a_{i}b_{i}
, and then we take
\Delta(G)+1
different number-based strings
a_{i}b_{i}
with
i\in[1,\Delta(G)+1]
for coloring properly the edges of
G
holding
\chi\,^{\prime}(G)\leq\Delta(G)+1
true, the remainder
N^{*}
different number-based strings are enough for properly coloring the vertices of
G
, by
k\leq\Delta(G)-\sqrt{3[1+\Delta(G)]}
, we have

N^{*}=M^{2}-\Delta(G)-1\geq 2\Delta(G)+2\geq\Delta(G)+1+K(G)\geq\chi(G)

where
K(G)
is the number of vertices of a largest clique of the graph
G
, we are done.
∎

There are several set-colorings and set-labelings introduced in
[
33
]
.

Definition 60
.

[
42
]
A
(p,q)
-graph
G
admits a set-labeling
F:V(G)\rightarrow[1,q]^{2}
(resp.
[1,2q-1]^{2})
, and induces an edge set-color
F(uv)=F(u)\cap F(v)
for each edge
uv\in E(G)
. If we can select a
representative

a_{uv}\in F(uv)
for each edge color set
F(uv)
such that
\{a_{uv}:~uv\in E(G)\}=[1,q]
(resp.
[1,2q-1]^{o}
), then
F
is called a
graceful-intersection (resp. an odd-graceful-intersection) total set-labeling
of
G
.
\square

Theorem 64
.

[
42
]
Each tree
T
admits a
graceful-intersection (resp. an odd-graceful-intersection) total set-labeling
.

We define a
regular rainbow set-sequence

\{R_{k}\}^{q}_{1}
as:
R_{k}=[1,k]
with
k\in[1,q]
, where
[1,1]=\{1\}
.

Theorem 65
.

[
42
]
Each tree
T
of
q
edges admits a
regular rainbow intersection total set-labeling
based on a
regular rainbow set-sequence

\{[1,k]\}^{q}_{k=1}
.

Proof.

Suppose
x
is a leaf of a tree
T
of
q
edges, so
T-x
is a tree of
(q-1)
edges. Assume that
T-x
admits a regular rainbow set-sequence
\{R_{k}\}^{q-1}_{1}
total set-labeling
f
. Let
y
be adjacent with
x
in
T
. We define a labeling
g
of
T
in this way:
g(w)=f(w)
for
w\in V(T)\setminus\{y,x\}
,
g(y)=R_{q+1}=[1,q+1]
and
g(x)=R_{q}=[1,q]
. Therefore, we have
g(u_{i}v_{j})=g(u_{i})\cap g(v_{j})=[1,i]\cap[1,j]
for
u_{i}v_{j}\in E(T)\setminus\{xy\}
, and
g(xy)=g(x)\cap g(y)=[1,q]
, and
g(s)\neq g(t)
for any pair of vertices
s
and
t
. We claim that
g
is a regular rainbow intersection total set-labeling of
T
by the hypothesis of induction.
∎

Remark 44
.

Each tree admits a regular odd-rainbow intersection total set-labeling based on a
regular odd-rainbow set-sequence

\{R_{k}\}^{q}_{1}
defined as:
R_{k}=[1,2k-1]
with
k\in[1,q]
, where
[1,1]=\{1\}
. Moreover, we can define a
regular Fibonacci-rainbow set-sequence

\{R_{k}\}^{q}_{1}
by
R_{1}=[1,1]
,
R_{2}=[1,1]
, and
R_{k+1}=R_{k-1}\cup R_{k}
with
k\in[2,q]
; or a
\tau
-term Fibonacci-rainbow set-sequence
\{\tau,R_{i}\}^{q}_{1}
holds:
R_{i}=[1,a_{i}]
with
a_{i}>1
and
i\in[1,q]
, and
R_{k}=\sum^{k-1}_{i=k-\tau}R_{i}
with
k>\tau

[
29
]
. It may be an interesting research on various rainbow set-sequences for non-tree graphs.
\parallel

.

38
is for understanding Theorem

, and we have a number-based string

&123456789101112\underline{12345678910111213}12345678\underline{12345678}\underline{1234567}1234567\\
&123456\underline{123456}12345\underline{12345}.\end{split}}

made by the totally-colored tree shown in Fig.

(b).
\square

6.5
Topcode-matrices with non-number elements

6.5.1
Topcode-matrices with the elements of strings, vectors and sets

Definition 61
.

[
35
]
Let
S_{x,i},S_{e,j}
and
S_{y,k}
be number-based strings with
1\leq i,j,k\leq q
, and put them into a set
G_{string}
. A
string-type Topcode-matrix

S_{code}(G_{string})
is defined by

\centering{\begin{split}S_{code}(G_{string})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}S_{x,1}&S_{x,2}&\cdots&S_{x,q}\\
S_{e,1}&S_{e,2}&\cdots&S_{e,q}\\
S_{y,1}&S_{y,2}&\cdots&S_{y,q}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}S_{X}\\
S_{E}\\
S_{Y}\end{array}\right)=(S_{X},~S_{E},~S_{Y})^{T}\end{split}}\@add@centering

(285)

and
S_{x,i}
and
S_{y,i}
are called the
ends
of
S_{e,i}
, and we call

S_{X}=(S_{x,1},S_{x,2},\dots,S_{x,q}),~S_{E}=(S_{e,1},S_{e,2},\dots,S_{e,q}),~S_{Y}=(S_{y,1},S_{y,2},\dots,S_{y,q})

string vectors
. Moreover the string-type Topcode-matrix
S_{code}
is
constraint valued
if there is a group of constraints
F_{1},F_{2},\dots,F_{n}
such that
S_{e,i}=F_{k}\langle S_{x,i},S_{y,i}\rangle
for some
k\in[1,n]
and each
i\in[1,q]
.
\square

Remark 45
.

About the string-type Topcode-matrix
S_{code}=(S_{X},~S_{E},~S_{Y})^{T}
defined in Definition
61
, we have

(i) Each
graph-based string

D_{k}(3q)=H_{k,1}H_{k,2}\cdots H_{k,3q}
with
k\in[1,(3q)!]
generated from a graphic Topcode-matrix
G_{code}
defined in Definition
28
holds each colored graph
H_{k,i}\in\{H_{x,i},H_{e,i}
,
H_{y,i}:i\in[1,q]\}
and
H_{k,i}\neq H_{k,j}
for
i\neq j
. Since the Topcode-matrix
T_{code}(H_{k,i})
of each
H_{k,i}
produces a number-based string
s(n_{k,i})
with length
n_{k,i}
, then we get a
number-based hyper-string

D_{k}=s(n_{k,1})s(n_{k,2})\cdots s(n_{k,3q})
with length
L_{\textrm{ength}}(D_{k})=\sum^{3q}_{i=1}n_{k,i}
for
k\in[1,(3q)!]
.

(ii) We have compressed a number-based hyper-string
D_{k}
with longer bytes into a graph-based string
D_{k}(3q)
with smaller bytes, more or less like the effect of Hash function.

(iii) The elements of a string-type Topcode-matrix
S_{code}
defined in Definition
61
may belong to an
every-zero string group

\{S(G);\oplus\ominus\}
.
\parallel

By Definition
55
, a
(p,q)
-graph
G

-constraint total vector-coloring

\centering{\begin{split}T_{code}(G,\overrightarrow{\pi})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\overrightarrow{\pi}(u_{1})&\overrightarrow{\pi}(u_{2})&\cdots&\overrightarrow{\pi}(u_{q})\\

\overrightarrow{E}\\
\overrightarrow{Y}\end{array}\right)=(\overrightarrow{X},\overrightarrow{E},\overrightarrow{Y})^{T}\end{split}}\@add@centering

with three vector-vectors

, a
(p,q)

-constraint total set-coloring

&\psi(u_{k}v_{k})=\{c_{k,1},c_{k,2},\dots,c_{k,m_{t}}\}\end{split}}

.

6.5.2
Non-number-type Topcode-matrices with parameters

For a set
S\,^{i}_{X}=\{a\,^{i}_{1},a\,^{i}_{2},\dots,a\,^{i}_{s(i)}\}

and

{\begin{split}&d\cdot S\,^{i}_{X}=\{d\cdot a\,^{i}_{1},d\cdot a\,^{i}_{2},\dots,d\cdot a\,^{i}_{s(i)}\}\\
&k\cdot I^{*}+d\cdot S\,^{i}_{X}=\{k+d\cdot a\,^{i}_{1},~k+d\cdot a\,^{i}_{2},~\dots,~k+d\cdot a\,^{i}_{s(i)}\}\end{split}}

(290)

where

is a unite vector.

Definition 62
.

[

]

. A connected bipartite
