is an odd-graceful labeling of the graph

.

We take a plane curve

defined on a domain

for

for

.

For each curve

has no solutions of integers when

.
\square

for users and the realization of “one-time one-secret password” or “one-time multi-secret passwords” or “multiple multi-secret passwords”.

We have infinite graphic groups for supporting the one-encryption one-time. In the MULTIPLE-JOIN algorithm, for example,

(i) By infinite graphic groups, we get every-zero infinite graphic groups

, and moreover there are every-zero infinite graphic group homomorphism

with

(\textbf{{H}}_{total})^{+\infty}_{-\infty}|^{2}~\sim~(\textbf{{T}}^{r}_{total})^{+\infty}_{-\infty}|^{2}

7.5.2
Self-certification topology algorithms

People customize some simple algorithms to encrypt their data. Usually, these custom algorithms make data more secure because they are different from common encryption algorithms.

For example, the users can replace topological signatures and topological Key-pairs by themselves, not letting the third party know. This is based on topological Key-pairs are irreversible, so that finding the topological signatures from the topological Key-pairs is impossible.

Another advantage of asymmetric topology encryption is its ability to serve as identification. If you encode a known string of data with your private-key string and attach it to a message, anyone who receives that message can decode it with your public-key string. Therefore, the encryption serves as a fingerprint, since only your private-key string could have encrypted the data. As known, the self-certified topology cryptography realizes the properties of no public-key certificate and the unmanaged public-key.

We design some self-certification topology algorithms with no third party and no managed public-key strings in this subsection.

Self-certification topology algorithm-I.

Self-1.
1.

Alice sends to Bob a key-package-A including Alice’s public-key string
s_{Apub}
and Alice’s public-key graph
G_{Apub}
(as the identity authentication).

Self-1.
2.

Bob sends to Alice a key-package-B including Bob’s public-key string
s_{Bpub}
and Bob’s public-key graph
G_{Bpub}
.

Self-1.
3.

Alice encrypts the plaintext
F
by Bob’s public-key string
s_{Bpub}
, and gets the encrypted
F\,^{*}_{1}
, and then Alice encrypts the encrypted file
F\,^{*}_{1}
by her private-key graph
G_{Apri}
, the resultant file is denoted as
F\,^{*}_{2}
with a 2-level protection.

Self-1.
4.

Bob, first, decrypts the encrypted file
F\,^{*}_{2}
by Alice’s public-key graph
G_{Apub}
, as the topological signature authentication
T_{sigA}=A_{uth}\langle G_{Apub},G_{Apri}\rangle
passed, the resultant file is denoted as
F\,^{\prime}_{1}
with a one-level protection only.

Self-1.
5.

Bob uses his private-key string
s_{Bpri}
to decrypt the encrypted file
F\,^{\prime}_{1}
, as the Key-pair string authentication
A_{uth}\langle s_{Bpub},s_{Bpri}\rangle
passed, then Bob gets the original plaintext
F
.

Remark 50
.

Since Alice’s public-key graph
G_{Apub}
and Bob’s private-key string
s_{Bpri}
are impossible to decipher, the self-certification topology algorithm-I has a high security; refer to Fig.
52
.
\parallel

Figure 52:
A scheme for the self-certification algorithm with no third party.

Self-certification topology algorithm-II.

Self-2.
1.

Alice sends to Bob a key-package-A including Alice’s public-key string
s_{Apub}
and Alice’s public-key graph
G_{Apub}
(as the identity authentication).

Self-2.
2.

Bob sends to Alice a key-package-B including Bob’s first public-key string
s^{1}_{Bpub}
, Bob’s second public-key string
s^{2}_{Bpub}
and Bob’s public-key graph
G_{Bpub}
.

Self-2.
3.

Alice, first, encrypts the plaintext
F
by Bob’s second public-key string
s^{2}_{Bpub}
, Alice’s private-key string
s_{Apri}
and Alice’s private-key graph
G_{Apri}
, the resultant encrypted file is denoted as
F_{3}
with a
3
-level protection.

Self-2.
4.

Alice makes Alice’s public-key string
s^{*}_{Apub}
by a assignment technique based one Bob’s first public-key string
s^{1}_{Bpub}
, and encrypts
F_{3}
by
s^{*}_{Apub}
, the resultant encrypted file is denoted as

with a

-level protection.

Self-2.
5.

Alice sends to Bob the encrypted file
F\,^{*}_{4}
and Alice’s public-key string
s^{*}_{Apub}
.

Self-2.

Bob, first, decrypts the encrypted file
F\,^{*}_{3}
by Alice’s public-key graph
G_{Apub}
, as the topological signature authentication
T_{sigA}=A_{uth}\langle G_{Apub},G_{Apri}\rangle
passed, the resultant encrypted file is denoted as
F\,^{*}_{3}
with a 3-level protection.

Self-2.

F\,^{*}_{3}
by Alice’s public-key string
s^{*}_{Apub}
and Bob’s first private-key string

A_{uth}\big\langle s^{*}_{Apub},s^{1}_{Bpub},s^{1}_{Bpri}\big\rangle
passed, the resultant encrypted file is denoted as
F\,^{*}_{2}
with a 2-level protection only.

Self-2.

Bob uses his first and second private-key strings
s^{1}_{Bpub}
and
s^{2}_{Bpri}
to decrypt the encrypted file
F\,^{*}_{2}
, after two Key-pair string authentications
A_{uth}\big\langle s^{1}_{Bpub},s^{1}_{Bpri}\big\rangle
and
A_{uth}\big\langle s^{2}_{Bpub},s^{2}_{Bpri}\big\rangle
passed, then Bob gets the original plaintext
F
.

Self-certification topology algorithm-III based on Key-graph sequences with the same ranks.

Self-3.
1.

Alice sends to Bob a key-package-A including Alice’s public-key string
s_{Apub}
, and Alice’s public-key graph sequence
\{G\,^{i}_{Apub}\}^{m}_{i=1}
(as the identity authentication).

Self-3.
2.

s_{Bpub}
, and Bob’s public-key graph sequence
\{G\,^{j}_{Bpub}\}^{m}_{j=1}
.

one
G\,^{i}_{Apri}
by one

passed, the resultant encrypted file is denoted as

.

For the security and computational complexity of the self-certification topology algorithm-III we have:

Scta-3.
1.

Attacking Alice’s public-key graph sequence
\{G\,^{i}_{Apub}\}^{m}_{i=1}
and Bob’s public-key graph sequence
\big\{G\,^{j}_{Bpub}\big\}^{m}_{j=1}
will meet the problems of topological structures and colorings at
m
times.

Scta-3.
2.

There is an order in encrypting the encrypted file
F_{1}
by Alice’s private-key graph sequence
\big\{G\,^{i}_{Apri}\big\}^{m}_{i=1}
, so the topological signature authentication
T\,^{i}_{sigA}=A_{uth}\big\langle G\,^{i}_{Apub},G\,^{i}_{Apri}\big\rangle
follows the topological signature authentication
T\,^{i+1}_{sigA}=A_{uth}\big\langle G\,^{i+1}_{Apub},G\,^{i+1}_{Apri}\big\rangle
, one-by-one.

Self-certification topology algorithm-IV based on Key-graph sequences with different ranks.

1.

passed, finally, Bob obtains the original plaintext
F
.

with

passed, finally, Bob obtains the original plaintext
F
.

8
Conclusion

In order to approach practical applications, resist the attack of quantum computing, and develop asymmetric topology cryptography, we have introduced a number of ways to induce various string as follows:

Meth
-1.

Number-based strings made by topological coding are as public-key strings.

Meth
-2.

Topcode-matrices produce number-based strings for public-key strings.

Meth
-3.

Colored graphs have Topcode-matrices, which induce number-based strings.

Meth
-4.

Vectors and sets from topological coding make number-based strings.

Meth
-5.

Compound number-based strings made by topological coding are as public-key graphs.

Meth
-6.

Number-based strings as Key-pairs are made by the integer partition and the integer decomposition.

Meth
-7.

[0,9]
-strings, and algebraic operations on
[0,9]
-strings, also,
string algebra
.

Meth
-8.

Number-based super-strings.

Meth
-9.

Self-breeding number-based strings.

Meth
-10.

Graph-based strings including total graph-colorings; TOTAL-graph-coloring algorithm-I; TOTAL-graph-coloring algorithm-II; complex number-based strings.

Meth
-11.

Number-based strings with parameters,plane-curve-attached strings, space-coordinate strings, fractional strings.

Meth
-12.

A
public-key graph

G
admitting a
W
-constraint parameterized coloring
F
can be used to make a
private-key graph

H=\langle G,F,f(x)\rangle
based on a real-valued function
y=f(x)
.

Meth
-13.

Assignment Topcode-matrices.

Meth
-14.

(abc)-linear colorings.

Meth
-15.

flawed
W
-constraint colorings and labelings.

Meth
-16.

Plane-coordinate string sequences.

Meth
-17.

Graphic lattices for making number-based strings including tree-base graphic lattices; tree-base
[0,9]
-operation graphic lattices.

Meth
-18.

Set-based strings.

Meth
-19.

Number-based strings from indexed-colorings including maximal planar graphic lattices; indexed-colorings; planar graphs and indexed-colorings.

Meth
-20.

Hanzi-based strings including planar graphs and Hanzi-based strings; Hanzi-Lattices; maximal planar graphs and Hanzi-based strings; the
[\odot_{\textrm{plan}}]
-Hanzi-graphic lattices.

Meth
-21.

Every-zero infinite graphic groups, every-zero infinite graphic group homomorphism, mutually equivalent infinite every-zero graphic groups.

We try to apply various strings to the actual scene:

Appl
-1.

Homogeneous number-based string-colorings, non-homogeneous number-based string-colorings, constructing number-based string-colorings.

Appl
-2.

Multi-level multi-rank strings.

Appl
-3.

Vector-colorings made by strings.

Appl
-4.

Set-colorings made by strings.

Appl
-5.

Topcode-matrices with non-number elements: Topcode-matrices with the elements of strings, vectors and sets; non-number-type Topcode-matrices with parameters.

Appl
-6.

For the overall encryption of networks, or the community encryption of different communities in a network, we have studied very-zero number-based string groups including GROUP-compound algorithm; Number-based sub-string groups; compound number-based string groups; Partial every-zero compound number-based string groups; and every-zero Topcode-matrix group.

Appl
-7.

Topcode-matrix algebra.

There are problems related with strings and string-colorings as follows:

Ques
-1.

An
[a,b]
-string

s=c_{1}c_{2}\cdots c_{n}
holds
c_{j}\in[a,b]
with two integers
a,b
subject to
0<a<b
. It is may be interesting to generalize the properties of
[0,9]
-strings to
[a,b]
-strings, and
find
some connections between
[a_{k},b_{k}]
-strings with integers
0<a_{k}<b_{k}
.

Ques
-2.

Given a number-based string
s=c_{1}c_{2}\cdots c_{n}
with
c_{i}\in[0,9]
and
c_{1}\geq 1
,
find
a positive integer
m
, such that
m
can be expressed as a sum
m_{i,1}+m_{i,2}+\cdots+m_{i,a_{i}}
defined in Eq.(
169
) of Definition
42
, and there is
s=s^{*}_{i}
defined in Eq.(
170
), or
s=s^{j}_{i}
defined in Eq.(
172
), or
s=s_{i,j}
defined in Eq.(
173
).

Ques
-3.

Given a number-based string
s^{*}=a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{m}
with
a_{i}\in[0,9]
and
a_{1}\geq 1
,
find
a positive integer
n
, such that
n
can be expressed as a product
p_{i,1}p_{i,2}\cdots p_{i,b_{i}}
defined in Eq.(
174
) of Definition
43
, and there is
s^{*}=r^{*}_{i}
defined in Eq.(
175
), or
s^{*}=r^{j}_{i}
defined in Eq.(
177
), or
s^{*}=r_{i,j}
defined in Eq.(
178
).

Ques
-4.

Let
G_{raph}(p)
be the set of graphs of
p
edges.
Find
a mapping
\theta:V(G)\rightarrow V(H)
for two graphs
G,H\in G_{raph}(p)
, such that there is a vertex
u\in V(G)
holding

G\wedge u\cong H\wedge\theta(u)
.

Ques
-5.

PRONBS-problem defined in Problem
1
is the parametric reconstitution problem of number-based strings.

Ques
-6.

ATE-CGH-problem defined in Problem
28
and Pan-ATE-CGH-problem defined in Problem
30
and ATE-To-CGH-problem defined in Problem
31
are the problems of asymmetric topology encryption related with colored graph homomorphisms.

The definition of “
zero knowledge proof
” in the blockchain is that the verifier can make the verifier believe that a certain assertion is correct without providing any useful information to the verifier.
Zero knowledge proof
is a more secure information verification or authentication mechanism. Security and privacy are the values of zero knowledge proof. We hope the techniques of asymmetric topology encryption, such as self-certification topology algorithms, can be applied into blockchains.

Assuming that attackers are very familiar with the technology of topology coding, how to generate Key-pairs and how to distribute Key-pairs without being cracked is one of the focuses of future research, although there is still a hard and challenging way to go from theory to practice.

provides
8!=40320
different Chinese sentences in total.
